Limits of Hodge Structures via holonomic D-modules
Qianyu Chen

Abstract
. We construct the limiting mixed Hodge structure of a degeneration of compact Kähler manifolds over the unit disk with a possibly non-reduced simple normal crossing singular central fiber via holonomic 𝒟 {\mathscr{D}} -modules, generalizing some results of Steenbrink. Our limiting mixed Hodge structure does not carry a ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} -structure; instead, we use sesquilinear pairings on 𝒟 {\mathscr{D}} -modules as a replacement. The limiting mixed Hodge structure can be computed by the cohomology of the cyclic coverings of certain intersections of components of the central fiber. Additionally, we prove the local invariant cycle theorem in this setting.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Limits of Hodge structures

Consider a degeneration of compact Kähler manifolds over the unit disk. The cohomology of each smooth fiber carries a polarizable Hodge structure. It is natural to ask how the family of Hodge structures on the cohomologies of smooth fibers degenerates and how the cohomology of the central fiber relates to that of nearby fibers. These are two classical and central questions in Hodge theory. Before Saito’s theory of mixed Hodge modules
[
Sai88
,
Sai90
]
, Schmid showed the existence of a limiting mixed Hodge structure for an abstract polarized variation of Hodge structures over the unit disk
[
Sch73
]
, using Lie theoretic methods. For the variation of Hodge structures coming from a semistable family of compact Kähler manifolds over a
1
-dimensional base, the limiting mixed Hodge structure was first established by Steenbrink
[
Ste76
]
. His construction is equivalent to Schmid’s in
[
Sch73
]
, but purely geometric.

In this paper, we construct the limits of Hodge structures for a family with the central fiber not necessarily reduced. The new feature of this paper is the usage of
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules, which makes everything less topological and more combinatorial. It also removes the need for using derived categories and semistable reduction.

Let us now give a more precise description of the problem and the main results. Let
f:X\to\Delta
be a proper holomorphic morphism from a Kähler manifold of dimension
n+1
onto the unit disk. Suppose that
0
is the only singular value and the central fiber
Y
of
f
has (possibly nonreduced) simple normal crossings. Denote by
X^{*}=X\setminus Y
and
\Delta^{*}=\Delta\setminus\{0\}
. Then the higher direct image of the relative de Rham complex
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X^{*}/\Delta^{*}}
over
\Delta^{*}
is a vector bundle, where the shifting is needed to adopt the convention of the theory of perverse sheaves and
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules; it underlies a polarizable variation of Hodge structure of weight
n
over the punctured disk
\Delta^{*}
. However, the higher direct image of the relative de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}
may not be a vector bundle when
Y
is singular. Steenbrink discovered a natural extension of the vector bundle
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X^{*}/\Delta^{*}}
over the origin via the relative log de Rham complex. Let

\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)=\Omega_{X}(\log Y)/f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)\text{ \,and\, }\Omega^{p}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)=\bigwedge^{p}\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y),

where
\Omega_{X}(\log Y)
(resp.
\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)
) is the sheaf of meromorphic one-forms on
X
(resp.
\Delta
) with log poles along
Y
(resp.
0
). Then the
relative log de Rham complex
is defined to be

\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)=\{{\mathscr{O}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\}[n].

Steenbrink showed in
[
Ste76
]
that
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is a locally free integrable logarithmic connection with a pole along the origin and its residue operator
R
has eigenvalues in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. It follows from the projection formula that there exists a canonical isomorphism

{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}(p)\simeq{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{X_{p}})

for every fiber
X_{p}
over any point
p\in\Delta
, where
{\mathbb{C}}(p)
denotes the residue field of
p
. Indeed, the vector bundle
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is Deligne’s canonical extension
[
Del70
]
of
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X^{*}/\Delta^{*}}
with eigenvalues of the residues of the log connection in the interval in
[0,1)
. We can think of the vector space
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
as a canonical specialization of
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X_{p},\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X_{p}})
for general fibers
X_{p}
.

Theorem

(Steenbrink)
.

Suppose that the central fiber
Y
is a reduced divisor with simple normal crossings. Then
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
underlies a limiting mixed Hodge structure with a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-structure which can be expressed in terms of the cohomology of certain intersections of components of
Y
via spectral sequences.

When
Y
is reduced, the residue operator
R
is nilpotent on the hypercohomology of
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
so it gives a monodromy filtration
W_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}(R)
on the vector space
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
uniquely characterized by two properties: (1)
R\cdot W_{\bullet}\subset W_{\bullet-2}
and (2)
R^{r}:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r}
is an isomorphism for every
r\geq 0
. The filtration
W_{\bullet}(R)
is called the monodromy filtration because
\exp\left(-2\pi\sqrt{-1}R\right)
is the monodromy induced by the generator of the fundamental group of
\Delta^{*}
. Steenbrink showed that the monodromy filtration is the weight filtration of the limiting mixed Hodge structure when
f
is projective, and this was later generalized to the Kähler case by Guillén and Navarro Aznar in
[
GNA90
]
.

Steenbrink further pointed out that his limiting mixed Hodge structure only depends on the log structure associated to the semistable family
f:X\to\Delta

[
Ste95
]
. Fujisawa extended Steenbrink’s results in
[
Ste76
,
Ste95
]
to semistable Kähler families over the polydisk and furthermore to the log geometry setting
[
Fuj99
,
Fuj08
,
Fuj14
]
. Recently, Nakkajima announced a simpler proof of Fujisawa’s results
[
Nak21
]
.

1.2.
Main results

We revisit Steenbrink’s theorem and construct the limiting mixed Hodge structure of the degeneration over the unit disk
\Delta
via the theory of holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules. One key difference with Steenbrink’s result is that we allow the central fiber
Y
to be nonreduced.

Theorem A
.

Suppose that the central fiber
Y
is a divisor with possibly non-reduced simple normal crossings. Let
R_{n}
(resp.
R_{s}
) denote the nilpotent (resp. semisimple) part of the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition of the residue operator
R
on the graded vector space
\bigoplus_{k}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
. Then, each eigenspace of
R_{s}
on

\bigoplus_{k,\ell}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})

underlies a limiting polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure over
{\mathbb{C}}
of central weight
n
, where
W_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}(R_{n})
is the monodromy filtration associated with
R_{n}
. In particular,
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
underlies a limiting mixed Hodge structure for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

A polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure is essentially a direct sum of polarized Hodge structures of different weights, preserved by an
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action. Refer to §
2.4
for the precise definition. In the context of Theorem
A
, the
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action is induced by the operator
R_{n}
and
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
, where
L=[\omega]\wedge
represents the Lefschetz operator for a Kähler form
\omega
. Specifically, each component
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
is a Hodge structure of weight
n+k+\ell
, and there are two Hard Lefschetz-type isomorphisms of Hodge structures:

•

\left(2\pi\sqrt{-1}L\right)^{k}:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{-k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})(k)
for
k\geq 0
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
;

•

R^{\ell}_{n}:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})(-\ell)
for
\ell\geq 0
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Our argument also says that the limiting mixed Hodge structure can be computed in terms of the cohomology of certain cyclic coverings of intersections of components of
Y
via spectral sequences.

As an application, we establish the local invariant cycle theorem, which is a key component in the Clemens-Schmid sequence
[
Cle77
]
, even when the central fiber
Y
is non-reduced.

Theorem B

(Local Invariant Cycle Theorem)
.

Suppose we are in the same setting as in Theorem
A
. Then the following sequence of mixed Hodge structures is exact:

H^{\ell}(Y,{\mathbb{C}}[n])\to{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}\left(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\right)\xrightarrow{R}{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}\left(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\right)(-1).

In particular, all cohomology classes that are invariant under the monodromy action come from the cohomologies of
Y
.

The local invariant cycle theorem was originally proven by Deligne in an algebraic setting for schemes
[
Del71
, Theorem 4.1.1]
, and later studied in
[
Ste76
]
,
[
Cle77
]
, and
[
GNA90
]
for semistable K”ahler degenerations. It was also generalized to the theory of mixed Hodge modules by Saito
[
Sai88
,
Sai90
]
.

1.3.
What is new

The traditional way to construct the limiting mixed Hodge structure, when the central fiber is not reduced, is to use the semistable reduction theorem. For example, Steenbrink mentioned his hypothesis on the central fiber is very general in
[
Ste76
, p230]
, and there is a more detailed explanation in his joint books with Peters
[
PS08
, 11.2.2]
. Mumford’s semistable reduction
[
KKMSD73
]
performs a sequence of base changes, normalizations, and blow-ups, that turns a given degeneration of compact Kähler manifolds over the unit disk into a semistable degeneration. This procedure is not canonical, and is hard to carry out in practice and it gives no information on how the non-reduced central fiber, especially the multiplicities of each component, affects the limiting mixed Hodge structure. Moreover, the information of eigenvalues of the monodromy will be lost after running semistable reduction.

By contrast, our approach via
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules is very direct and concrete: the local formulas for the
{\mathscr{D}}
-module are very simple and combinatorial so it is easy to do computations with them. We can construct the limiting mixed Hodge structure directly from the given degeneration, without using semistable reduction. In the process, we also get much more information about the action of the monodromy transformation, in particular about its eigenvalues and generalized eigenspaces. These contain interesting geometric information about the family, which is lost by going to a semistable reduction. For example, we can readily get the Jordan block of the residue operator. Also, it is obvious how the multiplicities of the central fiber affect the limiting mixed Hodge structure; in particular, we can see very clearly what the different eigenvalues are. Our approach also enables us to generalize the local invariant cycle theorem into the non-reduced case by local calculations.

1.4.
Strategy Overview

Steenbrink noticed that
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
can be seen as a realization of the nearby cycles
\psi_{f}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{X}[n+1]\right)
, which implies that the function
p\mapsto\dim{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{X_{p}})
is constant on
\Delta
. Thanks to Grauert’s theorem, it follows that the sheaf
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{\Omega}^{\bullet+n}{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is locally free. The logarithmic connection on
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
arises as the higher direct image of an operator
\nabla
fits into a distinguished triangle in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
:

f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}\otimes\Omega^{{}\bullet+n-1}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\xrightarrow{\nabla}f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}\otimes\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y).

Trivializing
f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}={\mathscr{O}}_{X}\cdot\frac{dt}{t}
, the induced operator

[\nabla]:\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\to\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}

has a characteristic polynomial whose roots lie in the interval
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
. The action of
[\nabla]
on the hypercohomology of
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
coincides with the residue operator
R
of the logarithmic connection. Therefore, the study of the monodromy filtration of
R
on the cohomology is equivalent to making the monodromy filtration of
[\nabla]
on the complex
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
explicit. However, one of the main challenges we face is constructing the monodromy filtration on the complex
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
since the operator
[\nabla]
exists only in the derived category. Steenbrink tackled this problem indirectly by resolving the relative log de Rham complex using a specific double complex, but this approach is limited to the case when
Y
is reduced. Additionally, he had to demonstrate that the monodromy filtrations are defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
through intricate topological arguments, ensuring that all the data yields a rational cohomological mixed Hodge complex.

Through the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence
[
Kas84
,
Meb84
]
, there exists a regular holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-module whose de Rham complex is isomorphic to
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
since the nearby cycle functor preserves perversity
[
Beĭ87
]
. This
{\mathscr{D}}
-module perspective allows us to derive the monodromy filtration easily through local calculations on a single
{\mathscr{D}}
-module, bypassing the need for derived categories. Furthermore, we provide a concrete description of the primitive parts of the associated quotient of the monodromy filtrations. Instead of relying on
{\mathbb{Q}}
-structures, we consider sesquilinear pairings on
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules, which serve as analogs of polarizations on Hodge structures. The polarization on the bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure in Theorem
A
is induced by a sesquilinear pairing. Although some topological information is lost, the sesquilinear pairings we use can be constructed purely algebraically and involve only symbolic calculations. The local calculations and the sesquilinear pairing support the choice of the monodromy filtration of
[\nabla]
as the appropriate weight filtration. Our method also naturally accommodates the case when
Y
is non-reduced. Now, let’s delve into the construction with more details.

We begin by providing an alternative proof of the fact that
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is locally free. Our approach relies solely on the eigenvalues of
[\nabla]
being in the interval
[0,1)
(Theorem
3.2
). Next, we proceed to translate the data of the relative log de Rham complex to the
{\mathscr{D}}
-module framework (see Section
4
):

Theorem C
.

There exists a filtered holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-module
({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}})
such that its de Rham complex
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
with the induced filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
is isomorphic to
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
with the stupid filtration in the derived category of filtered complexes of
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector spaces. Furthermore, there exists an operator
R:({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}})\to({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet+1}{\mathcal{M}})
whose eigenvalues are in the interval
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
, and under the above isomorphism,
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}R
can be identified with
[\nabla]
.

Then we investigate the Jordan block of the operator
R
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\geq\alpha}
(resp.
{\mathcal{M}}_{>\alpha}
) be the submodule of
{\mathcal{M}}
spanned by the generalized eigen-modules
\ker(R-\lambda)^{\infty}
for
\lambda\geq\alpha
(resp.
\lambda>\alpha
). Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}={\mathcal{M}}_{\geq\alpha}/{\mathcal{M}}_{>\alpha}
. Note that
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
is canonically isomorphic to
\ker(R-\alpha)^{\infty}
and therefore
R_{\alpha}=R-\alpha
acts nilpotently on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
. Using an idea of Saito
[
Sai90
]
, we filter
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
by

F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}=\frac{F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{\geq\alpha}+{\mathcal{M}}_{>\alpha}}{{\mathcal{M}}_{>\alpha}},\quad\text{ for }\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

The filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
is different from the naive one
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}\cap\ker(R-\alpha)^{\infty}
. The reason why we do not use the naive filtration is twofold:
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
provides the correct weight and is computationally more tractable. We proceed to prove that any power of the operator
R_{\alpha}
strictly respects the filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
. In other words, for every
\ell\geq 0
, we have the relation
R_{\alpha}^{\ell}F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}=F_{\bullet+\ell}{\mathcal{M}}\cap R_{\alpha}^{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
(Theorem
5.1
for the case
Y
is reduced and Theorem
7.5
for the general case).

This indicates that the monodromy filtration
W_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
and
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
interact very well. Note that the monodromy filtration associated to
R_{\alpha}
is the same as that of
R_{n}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
, which is the nilpotent part of the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition of
R
. We have the induced good filtrations

F_{\bullet}W_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}=F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}\cap W_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\quad\text{ and }\quad F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}=F_{\bullet}W_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}/F_{\bullet}W_{r-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}.

Denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,\ell}=\ker R_{\alpha}^{\ell+1}\cap{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
the
\ell
-th primitive for
\ell\geq 0
, which is isomorphic to

\frac{\ker R_{\alpha}^{\ell+1}}{\ker R_{\alpha}^{\ell}+\mathrm{im\,}R_{\alpha}\cap\ker R_{\alpha}^{\ell+1}}.

We endow it with the induced good filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,\ell}=\mathrm{im}\,(F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}\cap\ker R^{\ell+1}_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,\ell}).
A corollary of the strictness of every power of
R_{\alpha}
is that the Lefschetz decomposition of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
respects the good filtrations, i.e.

F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,-\frac{r}{2}\end{subarray}}R_{\alpha}^{\ell}F_{\bullet-\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,r+2\ell}.

See Theorem
5.6
for the case
Y
is reduced and Theorem
7.8
for the general case. This corollary suggests that it suffices to study the hypercohomology of each primitive part, as then the primitive parts serve as the source for the pure polarized Hodge structures.

We will construct a sesquilinear pairing
S_{\alpha}:{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}
using the Mellin transformation
[
Sab02
]
, where
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}}
is the naive conjugation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{X}
is the sheaf of currents. Both
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{X}
are canonically
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\overline{X}}
-modules where
{\mathscr{D}}_{\overline{X}}
denotes the sheaf of anti-holomorphic differential operators and the sesquilinear pairing is just a morphism of
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathscr{D}}_{X}}
-modules. The sesquilinear pairings on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
is an analogy of polarization on a Hodge structure: a complex polarized Hodge structure of weight
n
can be described as a filtered vector space
(V,F^{\bullet})
with a Hermitian pairing
S
such that
(-1)^{n-p}S
is a Hermitian inner product on
F^{p}\cap G^{n-p}
where
G^{n-p}
is the
S
-orthogonal complement of
F^{p+1}
. The sesquilinear pairing
S_{\alpha}
induces the second filtration on the hypercohomology of
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}
. For example, if
Y
is reduced, the pairing on
{\mathcal{M}}
is induced by

\mathrm{Res}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{\Delta}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\frac{d\bar{t}}{\bar{t}}\int_{X_{t}}:\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X},

where the constant scalar
{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{-(n+1)}}
depending on the dimension is used to make the pairing independent of the choice of orientation. The Mellin transformation is used here to extract the principal part of the asymptotic expansion of fiber-wise integration
\int_{X_{t}}:\omega_{X_{t}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\omega_{X_{t}}}\to\mathscr{C}_{X_{t}}
. We refer to the
\lx@sectionsign
2.1
for the definition of sesquilinear pairings on
{\mathscr{D}}
-module.

The operator
R_{\alpha}
is self-adjoint with respect to the pairing
S_{\alpha}:{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\alpha}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}
, i,e,
S_{\alpha}(-,R_{\alpha}-)=S_{\alpha}(R_{\alpha}-,-)
.
See
\lx@sectionsign
6
for the case that
Y
is reduced
\lx@sectionsign
8
for the general case. This implies we have an induced pairing on the associated graded modules:

S_{\alpha,r}:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}.

Then
P_{R_{\alpha}}S_{\alpha,r}=S_{\alpha,r}\circ\left({\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}R_{\alpha}^{r}\right)
defines a sesquilinear pairing on the primitive part
{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,r}
.

Theorem D
.

The graded vector space

\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(X,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,r})

together with the filtration induced by
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,r}
and the sesquilinear pairing induced by
P_{R_{\alpha}}S_{\alpha,r}
determine a polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n+r
with
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action induced by
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
.

A polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure basically is a direct sum of Hodge structures of different weights preserving by an
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action modeled by the direct sum of all the cohomology groups of a compact Kähler manifold. We refer to
\lx@sectionsign
2.3
for the definition of polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structures. To illustrate the idea of Theorem
D
, assume for a moment that
Y
is reduced and
Y=\sum_{i\in I}Y_{i}
where the
Y_{i}
’s are smooth components and
I
a finite index set. Then the endomorphism
R
is nilpotent and this implies that
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{0}
. Denote by
Y^{J}=\bigcap_{i\in J}Y_{i}
for any non-empty subset
J
of
I
. Let
\tau^{J}:Y^{J}\to X
be the closed embedding and
\tau^{(r+1)}:\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}=\coprod_{|J|=r+1}Y^{J}\to X
be the natural morphism for every
r\geq 0
. Put
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}={\mathcal{P}}_{0,r}
for simplicity. We will show that there exists a filtered isomorphism (Theorem
5.7
)

\phi_{r}:({\mathcal{P}}_{r},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}}_{r})\to\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}(-r).

Here, the Tate twist of a filtered
{\mathscr{D}}
-module is
({\mathcal{N}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{N}})(-r)=({\mathcal{N}},F_{\bullet+r}{\mathcal{N}})
. Moreover, the isomorphism respects the pairing
P_{R}S_{r}
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
(Theorem
6.5
):

P_{R}S_{r}(-,-)=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{(r+1)!}\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}S_{{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}}(\phi_{r}-,{\phi_{r}-}),

where
S_{{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}}
is the standard pairing on
\omega_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}
. Therefore,
{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(X,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{P}}_{r})
is isomorphic to
H^{n-r+k}(\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)},{\mathbb{C}})(-r)
as polarized Hodge structures of weight
n+r+k
. We get a polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n+r
with
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action induced by
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
on
\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(X,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{P}}_{r})
. For the case when
Y
is non-reduced, we will identify the primitive parts
{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha,r}
with certain filtered holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules coming from the cyclic coverings (Theorem
7.13
), and the identification also respects the sesquilinear pairing (Theorem
8.10
). As a direct consequence, we obtain

Theorem E
.

Let
V^{\alpha}_{\ell,k}={\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(X,{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{k}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha})
be the relabeling of the first page of the weight spectral sequence. Then
V^{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{k,\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V^{\alpha}_{\ell,k}
is a polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
with the polarization induced by
S_{\alpha}
and
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action induced by
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
and
R_{\alpha}
. Moreover, the differential
d_{1}
of the first page of the weight spectral is a differential of polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure.

By a formal argument of Guillén and Navarro Aznar
[
GNA90
]
, which follows some ideas of Deligne and Saito:

Corollary F
.

We have the following statements:

(1)

the Hodge spectral sequence degenerates at
\prescript{}{F}{E_{1}}
;

(2)

the weight spectral sequence degenerates at
\prescript{W}{}{E_{2}}
;

(3)

the
\alpha
-generalized eigenspace of the bigraded vector space

\prescript{W}{}{E_{2}}=\bigoplus_{\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(Y,\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})

with respect to
R
underlies a polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
with polarization induced by
S_{\alpha}
and
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-action induced by
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
and
R_{\alpha}
.

Note that the third statement in the above Corollary is equivalent to the Theorem
A
; therefore, we finish the proof of Theorem
A
. See Theorem
6.6
and Corollary
6.7
, when
Y
is reduced. See Theorem
8.11
and Corollary
8.12
, when
Y
is allowed to be non-reduced,.

1.5.
Outline

We first review basic notions on holonomic filtered
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules, integrable logarithmic connections and polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures in
\lx@sectionsign
2
. Then we set up the relative log de Rham complex and construct a log connection on its higher direct images in
\lx@sectionsign
3
. We transfer all of the data on the relative log de Rham complex into a filtered holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-module in
\lx@sectionsign
4
. To avoid the messy calculations, we first prove everything in the reduced case in
\lx@sectionsign
5
and
\lx@sectionsign
6
. The idea for the non-reduced case is almost the same but requires some Hodge theory of cyclic coverings. We construct some
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules in
\lx@sectionsign
7.4
as the summand of the primitive part and prove their hypercohomology underlie canonical polarized Hodge structures in
\lx@sectionsign
8.1
. Lastly, we prove the local invariant cycle theorem in
\lx@sectionsign
9
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Filtered
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules with sesquilinear pairings

We will work with right
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules unless further specified. Let
Z
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
and denote by
\Omega^{p}_{Z}
the sheaf of holomorphic
p
-forms and
{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}
the sheaf of holomorphic tangent vectors fields. For a filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module we mean a pair
({\mathcal{N}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{N}})
where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a coherent
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module and
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{N}}
is a good filtration. Occasionally, we will abuse notation and use
{\mathcal{N}}
to also denote the filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module when the filtration is clear. Denote by
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in Z}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
the associated graded algebra and
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{N}}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell}{\mathcal{N}}
the associated graded module. Note that
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{N}}
is a coherent
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module. Let
T^{*}Z=\mathrm{Spec}_{Z}\left({\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
be the algebraic cotangent bundle and
T^{*}_{V}Z
the geometric conormal bundle of a subvariety
V
in
Z
. The
characteristic variety
of
{\mathcal{N}}
is the support of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{N}}
on
T^{*}Z
and is denoted by
char({\mathcal{N}})
. The
characteristic cycle
of
{\mathcal{N}}
is the cycle associated to the coherent sheaf
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{N}}
on
T^{*}Z
and is denoted by
cc({\mathcal{N}})
. Neither the characteristic variety nor the characteristic cycle depends on the choice of filtration
[
HTT08
]
. For example, the canonical bundle
\omega_{Z}
is naturally a holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module with action

m.\xi=-d(\xi\righthalfcup m)

for local sections
\xi
of
{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}
and
m
of
\omega_{Z}
. It also naturally has a good filtration

(2.1)

F_{\ell}\omega_{Z}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \omega_{Z}&,&\ell\geq-n;\\
0&,&\ell<-n.\end{aligned}\right.

Then one can compute
cc(\omega_{Z})=[T^{*}_{Z}Z]
which is the cycle of the zero section of the cotangent bundle. We call
{\mathcal{N}}
a
holonomic

{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module if
\dim char({\mathcal{N}})=n
. See more details in
[
HTT08
]
. A
Tate twist
of filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module is defined to be
{\mathcal{N}}(-r)=({\mathcal{N}},F_{\bullet+r}{\mathcal{N}})
for any
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Denote by
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
the bounded derived category of complexes with values in finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector spaces and
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})
the bounded derived category of
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-modules. Denote by
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}_{h}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})
the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})
whose objects are complexes with holonomic cohomologies. For a morphism
f:Z\to W
between complex manifolds, denote by
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*},{\mathbf{R}}f_{!}:{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(W,{\mathbb{C}})
the derived pushforward and proper pushforward functors respectively and
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*},{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{!}
the
k
-th cohomology functors respectively. For any
{\mathcal{N}}^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})
, the pushforward functor and the proper pushforward functor
f_{+},f_{\dagger}:{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(W,{\mathscr{D}})
are by definition, respectively

f_{+}{\mathcal{N}}^{\bullet}={\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\left({\mathcal{N}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}}^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z\to W}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\dagger}{\mathcal{N}}^{\bullet}={\mathbf{R}}f_{!}\left({\mathcal{N}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}}^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z\to W}\right),

where
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z\to W}=f^{*}{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
is the transfer module. In fact, the functor
f_{\dagger}
preserves the holonomicty, i.e.,
f_{\dagger}:{\mathbf{D}}^{b}_{h}(Z,{\mathscr{D}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{b}_{h}(W,{\mathscr{D}})
(see
[
HTT08
]
). Of course, if
f
is proper or proper on the support of
{\mathcal{N}}
then
f_{+}=f_{\dagger}
. The
de Rham complex
of
{\mathcal{N}}
is

{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}\colon={\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{-\bullet}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}=\{{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{n}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{n-1}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{N}}\}

placed in degree
-n,\dots,-1,0
. If without further indication, tensor products are always taken over
{\mathscr{O}}
-modules. Some authors also call it the Spencer complex. The de Rham complex of
\omega_{Z}

\omega_{Z}\otimes\bigwedge^{-\bullet}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}=\{\omega_{Z}\otimes\bigwedge^{n}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}\to\omega_{Z}\otimes\bigwedge^{n-1}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}\to\cdots\to\omega_{Z}\}

is isomorphic to the usual de Rham complex
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}=\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}
of
Z
under the isomorphisms

(2.2)

\omega_{Z}\otimes\bigwedge^{p}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}\to\Omega^{n-p}_{Z},\,\,\,\omega\otimes\partial_{J}\mapsto(-1)^{n-j_{1}+\cdots+n-j_{p}}dz_{{\bar{J}}},

where
\partial_{J}
is a local section of
\bigwedge^{p}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}
,
J
is ordered index set and
{\bar{J}}
is the complement with the natural ordering, and
\omega=dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}
. If
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{N}}
is a good filtration, the de Rham complex is also filtered by

\displaystyle F_{\ell}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=F_{\ell+\bullet}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{-\bullet}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\{F_{\ell-n}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{n}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}\to F_{\ell-n+1}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\bigwedge^{n-1}{\mathscr{T}}_{Z}\to\cdots\to F_{\ell}{\mathcal{N}}\right\}.

The direct image functor and the de Rham functor commute
[
MS
, Corollary 4.4.4]
:

{\mathbf{R}}f_{!}\circ{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}={\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{W}\circ f_{\dagger}.

A
sesquilinear pairing

S
on
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}
-module morphism
S:{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{Z}
. Here,
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}={\mathscr{D}}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\overline{Z}}
for
\overline{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}}
is the sheaf antiholomorphic differential operators,
\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}
is the stupid conjugate of
{\mathcal{N}}
as a
\overline{{\mathscr{D}}}_{Z}
-module and
\mathfrak{C}_{Z}
is the sheaf of currents on
Z
with natural
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}
-module structure. We have the proper pushforward functor similarly as above on
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}
-modules and also call it
f_{\dagger}
:

f_{\dagger}(-)\colon={\mathbf{R}}f_{!}(-\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}}^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}\to W,\overline{W}}),

where
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z,\overline{Z}\to W,\overline{W}}\colon=f^{*}{\mathscr{D}}_{W,\overline{W}}
is the transfer module. Because of the natural morphism
f_{\dagger}\mathfrak{C}_{Z}\to\mathfrak{C}_{W}
, we can pushforward the sesquilinear pairing to get

{\mathscr{H}}^{0}f_{\dagger}S_{k}:{\mathscr{H}}^{k}f_{\dagger}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathscr{H}}^{-k}f_{\dagger}{\mathcal{N}}}\to{\mathscr{H}}^{0}f_{\dagger}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{W}.

If
f
is a closed embedding then
f_{+}S:f_{+}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}f_{+}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{W}
. If
W
is a point, then we have an induced pairing on the complex

f_{\dagger}S:{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}\to{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\mathfrak{C}_{Z}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[2n],

where
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}\simeq{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}}
. Taking cohomology at
0
-th degree gives, for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(2.3)

{\mathbf{H}}^{k}_{c}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}})\otimes{\mathbf{H}}^{-k}_{c}(Z,\overline{{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathcal{N}}})\to{\mathbf{H}}^{0}_{c}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}{\mathcal{N}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{N}}})\to H^{2n}_{c}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}.

Example 2.1
.

The
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module
\omega_{Z}
carries a natural pairing
S_{Z}:\omega_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\omega_{Z}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{Z}
,

(2.4)

\langle S_{Z}(m^{\prime},\overline{m^{\prime\prime}}),\eta\rangle=\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}\eta\cdot m^{\prime}\wedge\overline{m^{\prime\prime}},

for
m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
local sections of
\omega_{Z}
,
\eta
a test function on
Z
and
\varepsilon(n+1)=(-1)^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}
. The coefficient
\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}
in the definition is chosen so that
\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}m\wedge\overline{m}=|m|^{2}
is a positive current for any local section
m
of
\omega_{Z}
and elimination the choice of orientation (see more details in
\lx@sectionsign
2.3
). The pairing
S_{Z}:\omega_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\omega_{Z}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{Z}
yields a collection of pairings

{\mathbf{H}}^{k}_{c}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathbf{H}}^{-k}_{c}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})}\to{\mathbb{C}}.

2.2.
Logarithmic connections

If
D=\sum a_{i}D_{i}
is a simple normal crossing divisor on a complex manifold
Z
of
\dim Z=n
for
a_{i}\geq 0
, denote by
\Omega_{Z}(\log D)
the sheaf of meromorphic differential
1
-forms with logarithmic poles along
D_{\mathrm{red}}=\sum D_{i}
and denote by
\Omega^{p}_{Z}(\log D)=\bigwedge^{p}\Omega_{Z}(\log D)
the meromophic
p
-forms with logarithmic pole along
D
. Each
\Omega^{p}_{Z}(\log D)
is a locally free
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
-module.

In our convention, the
de Rham complex
of
Z
is
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}

\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}=\{{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}\to\Omega_{Z}\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{Z}\}[n].

The
log de Rham complex
is

\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}(\log D)=\{{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}\to\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{Z}(\log D)\}[n].

We will follow the Koszul sign rule: for a chain complex
C^{\bullet}
with differential
d
, the shifted complex
C^{\bullet+n}=C^{\bullet}[n]
equipped with differential
(-1)^{n}d
. We define residue along
D_{i}
by (see
[
EV92
, 2.5]
)

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{i}}:\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}(\log D)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n-1}_{D_{i}}(\log(D-D_{i})|_{D_{i}})\text{, }\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\wedge\alpha\mapsto\alpha|_{D_{i}},

where
z_{i}
is the local defining equation of
D_{i}
and
\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\wedge\alpha
is a local section of
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}(\log D)
. It factors through

\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}(\log D)|_{D_{i}}\to\Omega^{\bullet+n-1}_{D_{i}}(\log(D-D_{i})|_{D_{i}}).

By abuse of notations, we still call the above morphism
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{i}}
. Let
D^{J}=\cap_{j\in J}D^{J}
and
D_{J}=\sum_{j\in J}D_{j}
. Then we have a collection of residue maps, by choosing an order on the indices and successively applying
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{j}}
for
j\in J
,

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D^{J}}:\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{Z}(\log D)\to\Omega^{\bullet+\dim D^{J}}_{D^{J}}(\log(D-D_{J})|_{D^{J}}).

A
log connection

\nabla
with poles along
D
on a coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear morphism
\nabla:{\mathcal{F}}\to\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}
satisfying the Leibniz rule
\nabla(f\cdot s)=df\otimes s+f\nabla s
for
f
local section of
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
and
s
local section of
{\mathcal{F}}
. One can extend standardly
\nabla
to a log de Rham complex

\left\{{\mathcal{F}}\xrightarrow{\nabla}\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}\xrightarrow{\nabla}\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla}\Omega^{n}_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}\right\}[n].

If the above is a chain complex, i.e.,
\nabla^{2}=0
we say
({\mathcal{F}},\nabla)
is an
integrable
log connection. We call the morphism
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{i}}\nabla:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}|_{D_{i}}
induced by
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{i}}:\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\to{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}
the
residue
of the integrable log connection
\nabla
along
D_{i}
. Note that
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{D_{i}}
is
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
-linear and factors through again
{\mathcal{F}}|_{D_{i}}\to{\mathcal{F}}|_{D_{i}}
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
be the sub-algebra of
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
generated locally by the differential operators
P
such that
P\cdot{\mathscr{I}}_{D}\subset{\mathscr{I}}_{D}
. Here, we denote by
{\mathscr{I}}_{D}
the ideal sheaf of the normal crossing divisor
D
. An integrable log connection is then the same as a left
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-module. We can extend the definition of residues of a log connection as follows. The sheaf
{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}={\mathscr{O}}_{Z}/{\mathscr{I}}_{D_{i}}
naturally has a left
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-module structure because
{\mathscr{I}}_{D_{i}}
is also stable under by the
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-action by the naive reason. Let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
be a complex of integrable log connections. Then the complex

{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}}^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}

is a complex of
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-modules because taking tensor products over
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
is closed in the category of
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-modules and one can resolve either
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
or
{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}
using locally
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-free resolutions. The
\ell
-th cohomology
{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}})
is indeed an
{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}
-module equipped with an integrable log connection. The residue of this log connection is
{\mathscr{O}}_{D_{i}}
-linear and is called the
\ell
-th
residue
of the complex
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
.

As in the case of
{\mathscr{D}}
-module, the sheaf
\omega_{Z}(\log D)=\Omega^{n}_{Z}(\log D)
carries a canonical right
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-module structure and we have the left to right transformation
{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto\omega_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}
for any left
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}(\log D)
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Example 2.2
.

We will use the following fact: the complex of right
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-modules

\{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}\to\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}\}[n]

is a filtered resolution of
\omega_{Z}(*D)=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\omega_{Z}(kD)
, equipped the induced filtration by
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(\log D)\otimes F_{\ell+n+\bullet}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
. In fact, it is well-known that the inclusion
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(\log D)\to\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(*D)
is a filtered quasi-isomorphism
[
Del71
]
. The inclusion extends to a filtered quasi-isomorphism
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}\to\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(*D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
. Since
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(*D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
is a filtered resolution of
\omega_{Z}(*D)
, we conclude the proof. It follows that, for
f:Z\to W
,

f_{\dagger}\omega_{Z}(*D)={\mathbf{R}}f_{!}\left(\omega_{Z}(*D)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}}{\mathscr{D}}_{Z\to W}\right)=\left({\mathbf{R}}f_{!}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}(\log D)\right)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{W}.

In particular, if
f
is a closed embedding then
f_{!}=f_{+}
is exact and
f_{\dagger}={\mathscr{H}}^{0}f_{\dagger}
, which means

\{{\mathscr{D}}_{W}\to f_{+}\Omega_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{W}\to\cdots\to f_{+}\Omega^{n}_{Z}(\log D)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{W}\}[n]

is a resolution of
f_{\dagger}\omega_{Z}(*D)
. We put the induced filtration to make it a filtered resolution and denote by

f_{\dagger}(\omega_{Z}(*D),F_{\bullet}\omega_{Z}(*D))=(f_{\dagger}\omega_{Z}(*D),F_{\bullet}f_{\dagger}\omega_{Z}(*D)),

or for simplicity just
f_{\dagger}\omega_{Z}(*D)
.

The
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module looks like
{\mathcal{L}}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
for
{\mathcal{L}}
is a
{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}
-module is called
induced

{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module. For example, we have seen
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{Z}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
and
\Omega^{n+\bullet}(\log D)_{Z}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
are complexes of induced
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-modules.

2.3.
Polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structures

The goal of this subsection is to introduce polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures. Our treatment is an adjustment of
[
GNA90
, §4]
. The prototype of polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structures one should keep in mind is the graded vector space given by the direct sum of the cohomology groups of a compact Kähler manifold. Polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures are the degenerations of polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structures. We begin with the convention on Hodge structures and we only consider complex Hodge structures.

A
Hodge structure
of
weight

n
is a finite dimensional vector space
V
with two decreasing filtrations
F^{\bullet}
and
G^{\bullet}
satisfying

V=F^{p}\oplus G^{n+1-p},

for each
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The definition is equivalent to

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V^{p,q}

if we let
V^{p.q}=F^{p}\cap G^{q}
for
p+q=n
. A morphism of Hodge structures is just a morphism of vector spaces respecting the two filtrations
F
and
G
. A
polarization
on the Hodge structure
(V,F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})
is a non-degenerated hermitian pairing
S:V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that

(1)

F^{p}
is orthogonal to
G^{n+1-p}
with respect to
S
for every
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(2)

(-1)^{q}S(-,-)
is hermitian inner product on
V^{p,q}
.

Remark 2.3
.

A polarized Hodge structure of weight
n
is completely determined by the triple
(V,F_{\bullet}V,S)
because

G^{n+1-p}V=\left\{a\in V:S(a,\overline{b})=0\text{ for all }b\text{ in }{F^{p}V}\right\}=\overline{F^{p}V}^{\perp_{S}}.

We will also call the triple
(V,F_{\bullet}V,S)
a polarized Hodge structure.

A
Tate twist

(V,F^{\bullet},S)(r)
on a polarized Hodge structure
(V,F^{\bullet},S)
is the triple
(V,F^{\bullet+r},(-1)^{r}S)
, for any integer
r
.

Now let us move on to the geometric case. It is well-known that the
k
-th cohomology group of a compact Kähler manifold
Z
of dimension
n
has Hodge decomposition:

H^{k}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{p+q=k}H^{p,q}(Z)

and thus it is a Hodge structure of weight
k
. Fix a choice of
\sqrt{-1}
. Let
h
be any Kähler metric on
Z
. We denote the Kähler form by
\omega=-\mathrm{Im}\,h\in A^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{R}})
and denote its cohomology class by
[\omega]\in H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{R}})
; note that this depends on the choice of
\sqrt{-1}
through the function
\mathrm{Im}:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. The choice of
\sqrt{-1}
endows the two-dimensional real vector space
{\mathbb{C}}
with an orientation on
Z
. The induced orientation on
Z
has the property that

\int_{Z}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}=\mathrm{vol}(Z)>0.

The integral also depends on the orientation, hence on the choice of
\sqrt{-1}
. To remove the dependence, instead of the usual integral, we should use

\frac{1}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}:A^{2n}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}.

Let
{L=[w]\wedge}
be the Lefschetz operator for a Kähler class
[w]
. Then for
k\leq n
the primitive part

P_{L}H^{k}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\colon=\ker L^{n-k}\cap H^{k}(X,{\mathbb{C}})

is a polarized Hodge structure of weight
k
with the polarization

S(a,\overline{b})={\frac{\varepsilon(k+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}}\int_{Z}(2\pi\sqrt{-1}L)^{n-k}a\wedge\overline{b},

for
a,b\in P_{L}H^{k}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
because of the Hodge-Riemann bilinear relation.

If we consider the cohomology groups altogether, we will get the Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
. Denote by
(\mathsf{X,Y,H})
the
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-triple, i.e.,

[{\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Y}}]={\mathsf{H}},\quad[{\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{X}}]=2{\mathsf{X}},\quad[{\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{Y}}]=-2{\mathsf{Y}}.

In the Lie group
\mathrm{SL_{2}}({\mathbb{C}})
, we have the Weil element
\mathsf{w}=e^{\mathsf{X}}e^{-\mathsf{Y}}e^{\mathsf{X}}
with the property that
{\mathsf{w}}^{-1}=-{\mathsf{w}},
and under the adjoint action of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{{\mathbb{C}}})
on its Lie algebra, one has the identities

\mathsf{wHw^{-1}}=\mathsf{-H},\quad\mathsf{wXw^{-1}=-Y},\quad\mathsf{wYw^{-1}=-X}

From this, one deduces that
e^{\mathsf{X}}=\mathsf{w}e^{-\mathsf{X}}e^{\mathsf{Y}}=e^{\mathsf{Y}}\mathsf{w}e^{\mathsf{Y}}
. Now
A^{\bullet}(Z)
becomes a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{{\mathbb{C}}})
if we set

{{\mathsf{X}}=2\pi\sqrt{-1}L}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathsf{Y}}=(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{-1}\Lambda

and let
{\mathsf{H}}
act as multiplication by
k-n
on the subspace
A^{k}(Z).
The reason for this (non-standard) definition is that it makes the representation not depend on the choice of
\sqrt{-1}
. It is easy to see how
{\mathsf{w}}
acts on primitive forms. Suppose that
\alpha\in A^{n-k}(Z)
satisfies
{\mathsf{Y}}\alpha=0.
Then
{\mathsf{w}}\alpha\in A^{n+k}(Z).
If we now expand both sides of the identity

e^{{\mathsf{X}}}\alpha=e^{{\mathsf{Y}}}{\mathsf{w}}e^{{\mathsf{Y}}}\alpha=e^{{\mathsf{Y}}}{\mathsf{w}}\alpha

into power series, and then compare terms in degree
n+k,
we get

{\mathsf{w}}\alpha=\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{k}}{k!}\alpha.

This formula is the reason for using
{\mathsf{w}}
(instead of the otherwise
{\mathsf{w}}^{-1}
):
there is no sign on the right-hand side.

A Hodge-Lefschetz structure is linear algebra data encoding both representation theoretic and Hodge theoretic information. Recall that a finite-dimensional
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-representation is a graded vector space
V=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell}
with a set of endomorphisms
({\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Y}})
such that

(1)

each graded piece
V_{\ell}
is the
\ell
-eigenspace of
{\mathsf{H}}
;

(2)

the morphism
{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}:V_{-\ell}\to V_{\ell}
is an isomorphism for each
\ell\geq 0
;

(3)

the morphism
{\mathsf{Y}}^{\ell}:V_{\ell}\to V_{-\ell}
is an isomorphism for each
\ell\geq 0
.

Example 2.4
.

For any finite-dimensional vector space
V
together with a nilpotent operator
N
, there exists a so-called monodromy filtration
W_{\bullet}
uniquely determined by the following two conditions

•

for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
N:W_{\ell}\to W_{\ell-2}
;

•

the induced operator
N^{\ell}:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-\ell}
is an isomorphism for each
\ell\geq 0
.

Let
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}
. The
\ell
-th primitive part
P_{N}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}=\ker N^{\ell+1}\cap{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}
consists of the classes of generators of cyclic subspaces of
V
of dimension
\ell
as
{\mathbb{C}}[N]
-modules for
\ell\geq 0
. For each generator
v
, we have
N^{\ell+1}v=0
but
N^{\ell}v\neq 0
and also
v
is not a image of
N
. Therefore, we have the identification

P_{N}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}=\frac{\ker N^{\ell+1}}{\ker N^{\ell}+\mathrm{im\,}N\cap\ker N^{\ell+1}}.

Furthermore, we have the Lefschetz decomposition
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}N^{k}P_{N}V_{\ell+2k}
. Taking
N={\mathsf{Y}}
, the Lefschetz structure and the grading uniquely determines the operator
X
such that
({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Y}},{\mathsf{H}})
is a
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-triple by the relation
{\mathsf{X}}{\mathsf{Y}}^{k}=k(\ell-k+1){\mathsf{Y}}^{k-1}
on
P_{N}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}
. Thus
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}
naturally is a representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
.

By Hard Lefschetz theorem, for any compact Kähler manifold
Z
of
\dim Z=n
, the vector space
\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H^{n+\ell}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
is a representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
by setting
{\mathsf{X}}=2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
the Lefschetz operator,
{\mathsf{Y}}=(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{-1}\Lambda
the adjoint operator. But because of the Lefschetz operator of is of type
(1,1)
, we actually have
{\mathsf{X}}:H^{k}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{k+1}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})(1)
is a morphism of Hodge structures and
{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}:H^{n-\ell}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{n+\ell}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})(\ell)
is an isomorphism of Hodge structures. This leads to the following definition: a
Hodge-Lefschetz
structure of
central weight

n
is a
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-representation
V=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell}
with two filtrations
F^{\bullet}V=\bigoplus_{\ell}(F^{\bullet}V\cap V_{\ell})
and
G^{\bullet}V=\bigoplus_{\ell}(G^{\bullet}V\cap V_{\ell})
compatible with the grading such that

(1)

each graded piece
(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell})
is a Hodge structure of weight
n+\ell
;

(2)

the operator
{\mathsf{X}}:(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell})\to(V_{\ell+2},F^{\bullet+1}V_{\ell+2},,G^{\bullet+1}V_{\ell+2})
is a morphism of Hodge structures such that

{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}:(V_{-\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{-\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{-\ell})\to(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell})(\ell)

is an isomorphism of Hodge structures;

(3)

the operator
{\mathsf{Y}}:(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell})\to(V_{\ell-2},F^{\bullet-1}V_{\ell-2},G^{\bullet-1}V_{\ell-2})
is a morphism of Hodge structures such that

{\mathsf{Y}}^{\ell}:(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell})\to(V_{-\ell},F^{\bullet}V_{-\ell},G^{\bullet}V_{-\ell})(-\ell)

is an isomorphism of Hodge structures.

It follows from the definition the primitive part
P_{X}V_{\ell}
is a sub-Hodge structure for each
\ell<0
. Let
V_{\ell}=H^{n+\ell}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
and
V=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell}
. It follows that
V
is a Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
. Hodge-Lefschetz structure interplays well with the Hodge-Riemann bilinear relation. A
polarization
on a Hodge-Lefschetz structure
V
of central weight
n
is a hermitian symmetric paring
S:V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that

(1)

the restriction
S|_{V_{\ell}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V_{-k}}}
is zero for
\ell+k\neq 0
;

(2)

S({\mathsf{X}}-,-)=S(-,\bar{\mathsf{X}}-)
and
S(-,\bar{\mathsf{Y}}-)=S({\mathsf{Y}}-,-)
;

(3)

S_{\ell}:V_{\ell}\otimes\overline{V_{-\ell}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a polarization on
V_{\ell}
where
S_{\ell}:V_{\ell}\otimes\overline{V_{-\ell}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is the restriction of
S
.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
V
is a Hodge-Lefschetz structure, then
{\mathsf{w}}:V_{k}\to V_{-k}(-k)
is an isomorphism of Hodge structures of weight
n+k
.

Proof.

Suppose that
a\in V_{-\ell}
is primitive, in the sense that
{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell+1}a=0
and
\ell\geq 0
. Then
{\mathsf{Y}}a=0,
and from
{\mathsf{w}}e^{-{\mathsf{X}}}=e^{{\mathsf{X}}}e^{-{\mathsf{Y}}},
we get
{\mathsf{w}}e^{-{\mathsf{X}}}a=e^{{\mathsf{X}}}a,
and after expanding and comparing terms in degree
\ell-2j,
also

(2.5)

{\mathsf{w}}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j}}{j!}a=(-1)^{j}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell-j}}{(\ell-j)!}a

since
{\mathsf{w}}^{2}
acts on
V_{-\ell+2j}
as
(-1)^{-\ell+2j}=(-1)^{\ell},
this formula is actually symmetric in
j
and
\ell-j
.

Any
a\in V_{k}
has a unique Lefschetz decomposition

a=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j}}{j!}a_{j}

where
a_{j}\in V_{k-2j}
satisfies
Ya_{j}=0.
We only need to consider
j\geq k
in the sum because
{\mathsf{X}}^{2j-k+1}a_{j}=0,
which implies that
{\mathsf{X}}^{j}a_{j}=0
for
j<k
. Assuming
a\in V_{k}^{p,q}
, where
p+q=n+k
, we can show that
a_{j}\in V_{k-2j}^{p-j,q-j}
using descending induction on
j\geq\max(k,0)
. If
a_{j}\in V_{k-2j}^{p-j,q-j}
for
j>\ell
for some
\ell\geq\max(k,0)
, then we have

{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell-k}a=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j+\ell-k}}{j!}a_{j}=\sum_{j\geq\ell}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j+\ell-k}}{j!}a_{j}\in V_{2\ell-k}^{p+\ell-k,q+\ell-k}

This implies that
{\mathsf{X}}^{2\ell-k}a_{\ell}\in V_{2\ell-k}^{p+\ell-k,q+\ell-k}
, and therefore,
a_{\ell}\in V_{k-2\ell}^{p-\ell,q-\ell}
. In other words, the Lefschetz decomposition holds in the category of Hodge structures.

We can now check what happens when we apply
{\mathsf{w}}
. Using (
2.5
), we find that

{\mathsf{w}}a=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}{\mathsf{w}}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j}}{j!}a_{j}=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}(-1)^{j}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j-k}}{(j-k)!}a_{j}\in V_{-k}^{p-k,q-k}

and so
{\mathsf{w}}
is a morphism of Hodge structures. The same calculation shows that
{\mathsf{w}}^{-1}
is also a morphism of Hodge structures. It follows that
{\mathsf{w}}
is an isomorphism of Hodge structures.
∎

The following theorem implies that the definition of polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
can be simplified.

Theorem 2.6
.

A polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
on a filtered graded vector space
(V=\bigoplus_{k}V_{k},F^{\bullet}V)
is unique determined by the following:

(\mathrm{pHL1})

for each
\ell\geq 0
, we have an operator
{\mathsf{X}}:(V_{\ell},F^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell+2},F^{\bullet+1})
such that
{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}:F^{\bullet}V_{-\ell}\to F^{\bullet+\ell}V_{\ell}
is an isomorphism;

(\mathrm{pHL2})

a collection of Hermitian pairings
S_{\ell}\colon V_{\ell}\otimes\overline{V_{-\ell}}
for
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

S_{\ell}({\mathsf{X}}-,-)=S_{\ell+2}(-,\bar{\mathsf{X}}-);

(\mathrm{pHL3})

for
j\geq 0
, the triple
\left(P_{{\mathsf{X}}}V_{-j},F^{\bullet},S_{j}\circ\left({\mathsf{X}}^{j}\otimes{\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,}\right)\right)
is a polarized Hodge structure of weight
n-j
where
F^{\bullet}P_{{\mathsf{X}}}V_{-j}=\ker{\mathsf{X}}^{j}\cap F^{\bullet}V_{-j}
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially a linear algebra exercise, and we will only provide a sketch of the idea. Condition
(\mathrm{pHL1})
indicates that the Lefschetz decomposition associated to
{\mathsf{X}}
respects the filtration
F^{\bullet}
, using a similar approach as in the proof of Theorem
2.5
. Thus,
{\mathsf{Y}}
is uniquely determined and also respects the filtration. Condition
(\mathrm{pHL2})
implies that
S_{\ell}({\mathsf{Y}}-,-)=S_{\ell-2}(-,\overline{{\mathsf{Y}}}-)
. Condition
(\mathrm{pHL3})
implies that
S=\bigoplus_{\ell}S_{\ell}
is non-degenerate on
F^{p}V_{\ell}\otimes\overline{F^{p}V_{\ell}}
by the Lefschetz decomposition associated to
{\mathsf{X}}
on
F^{p}V
. Therefore, we can define the filtration
G^{q}V_{\ell}
as follows:

\displaystyle G^{q}V_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\{a\in V_{\ell}:S_{\ell}(a,\bar{b})=0\text{ for all }b\in F^{n-q+1}V_{-\ell}\}\quad\text{for}\quad q\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

It follows that
V^{p,q}_{\ell}=F^{p}V\ell\cap G^{q}V_{\ell}=\{a\in F^{p}V_{\ell}:S_{\ell}(a,\bar{b})=0\text{ for all }b\in F^{p-\ell+1}V_{-\ell}\}
for
p+q=n+\ell
gives us the Hodge decomposition
V_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n+\ell}V^{p,q}_{\ell}
.

Finally, we need to show that
(-1)^{q}S_{\ell}\circ({\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,}\otimes\bar{\mathsf{w}})
is Hermitian positive on
V_{\ell}^{p,q}
by condition
(\mathrm{pHL3})
. Let
a\in V^{p,q}_{\ell}
have the Lefschetz decomposition

a=\sum_{j\geq\max(\ell,0)}\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{j}}{j!}a_{j}

where
a_{j}\in P_{\mathsf{X}}V_{\ell-2j}^{p-j,q-j}
. Then,

(-1)^{q}S_{\ell}(a,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}a})=\sum_{j\geq\max(\ell,0)}(-1)^{q-j}S_{\ell-2j}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{X}}^{2j-\ell}}{j!}a_{j},\frac{1}{(j-k)!}\overline{a_{j}}\right)\geq 0

and the equality holds if and only if
a_{j}=0
for all
j\geq\max(\ell,0)
.
∎

Example 2.7
.

Let
Z
be a compact Kähler manifold of dimension
n
, and define
V_{\ell}=H^{n+\ell}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
and
V=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell}
. Then,
V
together with
{\mathsf{X}}=2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
and
{\mathsf{Y}}=(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{-1}\Lambda
, along with the natural filtration, forms a Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
. By the Hodge-Riemann bilinear relation, the sesquilinear pairing

(2.6)

S_{\ell}(a,\bar{b})=\frac{\varepsilon(n-\ell+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}a\wedge\bar{b}=\varepsilon(\ell)(-1)^{\ell n}\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}a\wedge\bar{b}

for
a\in V_{\ell}
and
b\in V_{-\ell}
gives a polarization on
V
. The point of this example is to show that the polarized Hodge-Lefschetz structure
V
is determined by the filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{Z}
-module
\omega_{Z}
together with the sesquilinear pairing
S_{Z}
as defined in (
2.4
). The graded piece
V_{\ell}
is
{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})
, with the induced filtration
F^{\bullet}V_{\ell}
given by the image of
{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(Z,F_{-\bullet}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})
. Now we work out the relation between
S_{k}
and
S_{Z}
explicitly. First, the sesquilinear pairing
S_{Z}
induces a map:

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{k}\colon{\mathbf{H}}^{k}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})\otimes\overline{{\mathbf{H}}^{-k}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z})}\to{\mathbf{H}}^{0}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\omega_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\omega_{Z}})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{S_{Z}}{\mathbf{H}}^{0}(Z,{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\mathfrak{C}_{Z})\simeq{\mathbb{C}},

and we have to make
S^{\prime}_{k}
clear. It is more convenient for us to use distribution in the situation by the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\omega_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\omega_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\mathfrak{C}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z,\overline{Z}}\mathfrak{Db}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

where the upper horizontal arrow is the isomorphism induced by (
2.2
) and similarly the lower horizontal arrow is defined on the terms in degree
-k,

\mathfrak{C}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Z,\bar{Z}}}\bigwedge^{k}\mathscr{T}_{Z,\bar{Z}}\to\Omega_{Z,\bar{Z}}^{2n-k}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Z,\bar{Z}}}\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{b}_{Z}

by the following rule: write a current locally as
D\omega\wedge\bar{\omega},
with a distribution
D
and denote by
\partial_{J}=\bigwedge_{J}\partial_{j}
and
dx_{\bar{J}}=\bigwedge_{i\notin J}dx_{i}
for an ordered index subset
J
of
I
; then

(2.7)

(D\omega\wedge\bar{\omega})\otimes\partial_{J}\wedge\bar{\partial}_{K}\mapsto(-1)^{\left(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{p}\right)+\left(k_{1}+\cdots+k_{q}\right)}(-1)^{nq}dx_{\bar{J}}\wedge\overline{dx}_{\bar{K}}\otimes D

where
|J|=p
and
|K|=q,
and
p+q=k.
The sign factor is explained by the number of swaps that are needed to move everything into the right place, which is
\left(2n-j_{1}\right)+\cdots+\left(2n-j_{p}\right)+\left(n-k_{1}\right)+\cdots+\left(n-k_{q}\right)
.
We can now derive a formula for the induced pairing

(2.8)

{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}}\to\mathrm{DR}_{Z,\bar{Z}}\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{b}_{Z}.

For the two local sections
\alpha=dx_{\bar{J}}
and
\beta=dx_{\bar{K}}
, under the isomorphism
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}{\mathscr{O}}_{Z}\cong{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z}
in (
2.2
), the
(n-p)
-form

\alpha\mapsto(-1)^{np}(-1)^{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{p}}\cdot\omega\otimes\partial_{J}.

and the
(n-q)
-form

\beta\mapsto(-1)^{nq}(-1)^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{q}}\cdot\omega\otimes\partial_{K}.

The pairing
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}S_{Z}
on
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{Z}\omega_{Z}
takes those two sections to

(2.9)

(-1)^{n(p+q)}(-1)^{\left(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{p}\right)+\left(k_{1}+\cdots+k_{q}\right)}S_{Z}(\omega,\omega)\otimes\partial_{J}\wedge\bar{\partial}_{K}.

Now
S_{Z}(\omega,\omega)=D_{Z}\omega\wedge\bar{\omega},
where
D_{Z}
is the distribution

D_{Z}=\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}

Under the isomorphism in (
2.7
) the section (
2.9
) therefore goes to

(-1)^{np}dx_{\bar{J}}\wedge\overline{dx}_{\bar{K}}\otimes D_{Z}=(-1)^{n(\operatorname{deg}\alpha-n)}\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta}\otimes D_{Z}

The formula we have just derived also works for smooth forms, of course. In other words, the same formula can be used to extend (
2.8
) to a pairing on the de Rham complex of smooth forms. The resulting pairings on cohomology are

(2.10)

S^{\prime}_{k}\colon H^{n+k}(Z,\mathbb{C})\otimes\overline{H^{n-k}(Z,\mathbb{C})}\to\mathbb{C},\quad(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(-1)^{n(\operatorname{deg}\alpha-n)}\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{Z}\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta}.

Comparing with the pairing (
2.6
), we see that the polarization
S_{k}=\varepsilon(k)S^{\prime}_{k}
.

2.4.
Polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures

Heuristically, what we really consider is the degeneration of the “variation of Hodge-Lefschetz structures” of a family of compact Kähler manifolds and as it turns out the limit of the degeneration is a bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure. We begin to define polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures, which is an adjustment of
[
GNA90
, §4]
.
Similarly to the case of
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-representation, a
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-representation is a bigraded vector space
V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell,k}
with two sets of endomorphisms
({\mathsf{X}}_{1},{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},{\mathsf{H}}_{1})
and
({\mathsf{X}}_{2},{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},{\mathsf{H}}_{2})
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

any member in the set
({\mathsf{X}}_{1},{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},{\mathsf{H}}_{1})
commutes with any member in the set
({\mathsf{X}}_{2},{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},{\mathsf{H}}_{2})
;

(2)

each bigraded piece
V_{\ell,k}
is the
\ell
-th eigenspace of
{\mathsf{H}}_{1}
and
k
-th eigenspace of
{\mathsf{H}}_{2}
;

(3)

we have
{\mathsf{X}}_{1}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{\ell+2,k}
and
{\mathsf{X}}_{2}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{\ell,k+2}
for each
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and

{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}_{1}:V_{-\ell,k}\to V_{\ell,k}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{X}}^{k}_{2}:V_{\ell,-k}\to V_{\ell,k}

are isomorphisms for
\ell,k\geq 0
;

(4)

we have
{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{\ell-2,k}
and
{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{\ell,k-2}
for each
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and

{\mathsf{Y}}^{\ell}_{1}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{-\ell,k}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{Y}}^{k}_{2}:V_{\ell,k}\to V_{\ell,-k}

are isomorphisms for
\ell,k\geq 0
.

A
bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure
of
central weight

n
is a
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-representation
V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell,k}
with two filtrations
F^{\bullet}V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k}F^{\bullet}V\cap V_{\ell,k}
and
G^{\bullet}V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k}G^{\bullet}V\cap V_{\ell,k}
compatible with the bigrading such that

(1)

the bifiltered vector space
(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet}V_{\ell,k},G^{\bullet}V_{\ell,k})
is a Hodge structure of weight
n+\ell+k
;

(2)

the two operators

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{X}}_{1}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell+2,k},F^{\bullet+1},G^{\bullet+1})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{X}}_{2}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,k+2},F^{\bullet+1},G^{\bullet+1})

are morphisms of Hodge structures such that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}_{1}:(V_{-\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})(\ell)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{X}}^{k}_{2}:(V_{\ell,-k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})(k)

are isomorphisms of Hodge structures for
\ell,k\geq 0
.

(3)

the two operators

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}}_{1}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell-2,k},F^{\bullet-1},G^{\bullet-1})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}}_{2}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,k-2},F^{\bullet-1},G^{\bullet-1})

are morphisms of Hodge structures such that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}}^{\ell}_{1}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{-\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})(-\ell)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}}^{k}_{2}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,-k},F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet})(-k)

are isomorphisms of Hodge structures for
\ell,k\geq 0
.

A
polarization
on a bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure
V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{\ell,k}
of central weight
n
is a Hermitian symmetric pairing
S:V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that

(1)

the restriction
S|_{V_{\ell,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V_{i,j}}}\colon V_{\ell,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V_{i,j}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
vanishes unless for
\ell=-i
and
k=-j
;

(2)

S({\mathsf{X}}_{1}-,-)=S(-,\overline{{\mathsf{X}}_{1}}-)
and
S(-,\overline{{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}}-)=S({\mathsf{Y}}_{2}-,-)
;

(3)

S_{\ell,k}(-,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}}-)
is a polarization on
V_{\ell,k}
, where
S_{\ell,k}
is the restriction of
S
on
V_{\ell,k}\otimes\overline{V_{-\ell,k}}
and
{\mathsf{w}}_{i}=e^{{\mathsf{X}}_{i}}e^{-{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}}e^{{\mathsf{X}}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.

This is the practical definition because in the later application,
{\mathsf{X}}_{1}
will be the
2\pi\sqrt{-1}L
and
{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}
will be, up to a scalar, the logarithmic of the monodromy for the degeneration. Similarly to the case of Hodge-Lefschetz structure Theorem
2.6
, we have a simpler definition.

Theorem 2.8
.

A polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure of central weight
n
on a filtered bigraded vector space
(V=\bigoplus_{\ell,k}V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet}V)
is uniquely determined by the following:

(\mathrm{pbHL1})

there are two commuting operators
{\mathsf{X}}_{1}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell+2,k},F^{\bullet+1})
and
{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}:(V_{\ell,k},F^{\bullet})\to(V_{\ell,k-2},F^{\bullet-1})
such that, for every
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}_{1}:F^{\bullet}V_{-\ell,k}\to F^{\bullet+\ell}V_{\ell,k}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{Y}}^{k}_{2}:F^{\bullet}V_{\ell,k}\to F^{\bullet-k}V_{\ell,-k}\text{ are isomorphisms};

(\mathrm{pbHL2})

a collection of Hermitian pairings
S_{\ell,k}:V_{\ell,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V_{-\ell,-k}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that

S_{\ell,k}({\mathsf{X}}_{1}-,-)=S_{\ell+2,k}(-,\overline{{\mathsf{X}}_{1}}-)\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\ell,k}(-,\overline{{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}}-)=S_{\ell,k-2}({\mathsf{Y}}_{2}-,-);

(\mathrm{pbHL3})

the triple
\left(P_{-\ell,k},F^{\bullet}P_{-\ell,k},S_{\ell,-k}\circ\left({\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}_{1}\otimes\left(-\overline{{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}}\right)^{k}\right)\right)
is a polarized Hodge structure of weight
n-\ell+k
where
F^{\bullet}P_{-\ell,k}=\ker{\mathsf{X}}^{\ell}_{1}\cap\ker{\mathsf{Y}}^{k}_{2}\cap F^{\bullet}V_{-\ell,k}
is the bi-primitive part.

Then the
G^{q}V_{j,k}
can be described as

\displaystyle G^{q}V_{j,k}

\displaystyle=\{a\in V_{j,k}\colon S_{j,k}(a,\bar{b})=0\text{ for all }b\in F^{n-q+1}V_{-j,-k}\}\quad\text{for}\quad q\in{\mathbb{Z}}

and

V^{p,q}_{j,k}=\{a\in F^{p}V_{j,k}\colon S_{j,k}(a,\bar{b})=0\text{ for all }b\in F^{p-j-k+1}V_{-j,-k}\},\quad\text{for}\quad p+q=n+j+k.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
2.6
and is left to the readers. Later when we construct the limiting mixed Hodge structure, the polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure naturally comes up from the first page of the weight spectral sequence associated to a mixed Hodge complex. Modeled on the properties of the differential of spectral sequence we give the following definition:

A
differential
of a polarized bigraded Hodge Lefschetz structure
(V,F^{\bullet},{\mathsf{X}}_{1},{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},S)
is a linear map
d:V\to V
such that

(1)

d:(V_{j,k},F^{\bullet})\to(V_{j+1,k-1},F^{\bullet})
and
d^{2}=0
;

(2)

d
is skew-symmetric with respect to
S
, i.e.,
S(d-,-)+S(-,\bar{d}-)=0
;

(3)

[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d]=0
and
[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d]=0
.

Remark 2.9
.

In fact, the above three conditions imply that
d
is a morphism of Hodge structures
d:V^{p,q}_{j,k}\to V^{p,q}_{j+1,k-1}
. A vector
a\in G^{q}V_{j,k}
means that
S(a,\bar{b})=0
for all
b\in F^{n-q+1}V_{-j,-k}
. Then
S(da,b)=S(a,db)=0
for all
b\in F^{n-q+1}V_{-j-1,-k+1}
, indicating
da
belongs to
G^{q}V_{j+1,k-1}
.

The main result of this subsection is the following version of Deligne’s lemma, shown by Guillén and Navarro Aznar.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
GNA90
, (4.5)]
)
.

The cohomology
\ker d/\mathrm{im}\,d
of a polarized differential bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure is again a polarized bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure.

Proof.

Let
C:V\to V
be the operator that acts as
(-1)^{q}
on the subspace
V_{j,k}^{p,q}
in the Hodge decomposition of each
V_{j,k}.
Since
d
is a morphism of Hodge structures, we have
[d,C]=0
. The fact that
S
is a polarization means that the Hermitian pairing

h^{+}:V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}\to\mathbb{C},\quad h^{+}(a,\bar{b})=S\left(Ca,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}b}\right)

is positive-definite on
V
. Let
d^{*}
be the adjoint of
d
with respect to
h^{+}
. Fix
a\in V_{j,k}
and
b\in V_{j,k},
:

\displaystyle h^{+}(da,\bar{b})

\displaystyle=S\left(Cda,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}b}\right)=S\left(dCa,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}b}\right)

\displaystyle=-S\left(Ca,\overline{d{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}b}\right)=-S\left(Ca,\overline{{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}\cdot{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{-1}{\mathsf{w}}_{1}^{-1}d{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}\cdot b}\right)=h^{+}\left(a,\overline{d^{*}b}\right),

i.e. the adjoint
d^{*}=-{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{-1}{\mathsf{w}}_{1}^{-1}d{\mathsf{w}}_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}
.

In addition to the two relations in the definition of differential

\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d\right]=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d\right]=0

we obtain from the grading another two relations

\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{1},d\right]=d\quad\text{ and }\quad\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{2},d\right]=-d.

This means, with respect to the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-action on
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V),
the element
d
has weight
(+1,-1),
and is primitive with respect to the action by
{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}
and
{\mathsf{X}}_{2}.
Define

d_{1}=\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},d\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad d_{2}=-\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{2},d\right].

The reason for the minus sign is that we have
\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d\right]=0
. Then
d_{1}
has weight
(-1,-1),
and is primitive with respect to the action by
{\mathsf{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathsf{X}}_{2}
, i.e.
\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},d_{1}\right]=0
and
\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d_{1}\right]=0
; this gives

{\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{1},d_{1}\right]=-d_{1},}\quad{\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{2},d_{1}\right]=-d_{1},}\quad{\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d_{1}\right]=d},\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{1}d_{1}{\mathsf{w}}_{1}^{-1}=d.

Similarly,
d_{2}
has weight
(+1,+1),
and therefore

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{2},d_{2}\right]=d_{2},}&{\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{2},d_{2}\right]=0,\quad\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d_{2}\right]=-d,\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{2}d_{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{-1}=d}\\
{\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{1},d_{2}\right]=d_{2},}&{\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d_{2}\right]=0}.\end{array}

Therefore,
d^{*}=-\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},d_{2}\right]=\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{2},d_{1}\right]\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}V
. It has weight
(-1,+1),
and is primitive with respect to
{\mathsf{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}
i.e
\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},d^{*}\right]=0
and
\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{2},d^{*}\right]=0
. From this, and the identities we already have, we deduce the following set of relations:

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{1},d^{*}\right]=-d^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=-d_{2},\quad

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{1},d^{*}\right]=0,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{w}}_{1}d^{*}{\mathsf{w}}_{1}^{-1}=-d_{2}

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{H}}_{2},d^{*}\right]=d^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{2},d^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=0,\quad

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{Y}}_{2},d^{*}\right]=d_{1},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{w}}_{2}d^{*}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{-1}=-d_{1}.

We can check that the (formal) Laplace operator

\Delta=dd^{*}+d^{*}d\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)

is invariant under the action of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).
For example,

\begin{array}[]{l}{\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},dd^{*}\right]={\mathsf{X}}_{1}dd^{*}-dd^{*}{\mathsf{X}}_{1}=d{\mathsf{X}}_{1}d^{*}-d\left({\mathsf{X}}_{1}d^{*}+d_{2}\right)=-dd_{2}}\\
{\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},d^{*}d\right]={\mathsf{X}}_{1}d^{*}d-d^{*}d{\mathsf{X}}_{1}=\left(d^{*}{\mathsf{X}}_{1}-d_{2}\right)d-d^{*}{\mathsf{X}}_{1}d=-d_{2}d}\end{array}

from which we conclude, using
d^{2}=0,
that

\left[{\mathsf{X}}_{1},\Delta\right]=-\left(dd_{2}+d_{2}d)=-(d\left(d{\mathsf{X}}_{2}-{\mathsf{X}}_{2}d\right)+\left(d{\mathsf{X}}_{2}-{\mathsf{X}}_{2}d\right)d\right)=0

The other three commutators can be checked similarly. On the other hand,
\Delta
is also a morphism of Hodge structures: the reason is that

d:V_{j,k}\to V_{j+1,k-1},\quad{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}:V_{j,k}\to V_{j-2,k}(-1),\quad{\mathsf{X}}_{2}:V_{j,k}\to V_{j,k+2}(1)

are all morphisms of Hodge structures, and
\Delta
is obtained by composing them in some order. It follows that
\ker\Delta\subseteq V
is a bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structure, polarized by the restriction of
S
. Because of the canonical isomorphism
\ker\Delta\simeq\ker d/\mathrm{im\,}d
as bigraded Hodge-Lefschetz structures, the induced pairing by
S
on
\ker d/\mathrm{im\,}d
is also a polarization. This concludes the proof.
∎

3.
Relative log de Rham complex

Let
f:X\to\Delta
be a proper holomorphic morphism smooth away from the origin whose central fiber
Y
is simple normal crossing but not necessarily reduced. Assume
X
is Kähler of dimension
n+1
and
Y=\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}Y_{i}
where
Y_{i}
’s are smooth components and
I
a finite index set. Let
t
be a parameter on
\Delta
and
z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
a local coordinate system on
X
such that
t=z^{e_{0}}_{0}z^{e_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{e_{k}}_{k}
such that
e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\geq 1
. Then we have
\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)={\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}\cdot\frac{dt}{t}
and
\Omega_{X}(\log Y)
is locally generated by

e_{0}\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}},e_{1}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}},\dots,e_{k}\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}},dz_{k+1},dz_{k+2},\dots,dz_{n}

over
{\mathscr{O}}_{X}
. Denote by
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
the classes of the above generators in
\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
, respectively. As a quotient of
\Omega_{X}(\log Y)
, the sheaf
\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is generated by
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
, but under the relation
\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n}=0
because

f^{*}\frac{dt}{t}=e_{0}\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}}+e_{1}\frac{dz_{1}}{dz_{1}}+\cdots+e_{k}\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}

is in
f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)
. Let
{\mathscr{T}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
be the dual bundle of
\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
. Then
{\mathscr{T}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is a subsheaf of
{\mathscr{T}}_{X}
, generated by

(3.11)

D_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}&,&1\leq i\leq k\\
\partial_{i}&,&i>k,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\partial_{i}
is the local section of
{\mathscr{T}}_{X}
dual to
dz_{i}
in
\Omega_{X}
. It follows that
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
is the dual frame of
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{n}
.

3.1.
A “log connection”

We shall construct an endomorphism of
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})
, which should be regarded a “log connection”. The short exact sequence of
{\mathscr{O}}_{X}
-modules

0\to f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to 0,

under the identification
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge:{\mathscr{O}}_{X}\to f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)
