, can be expressed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{dt}{t}\wedge}

Here, the morphism
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge:\Omega^{k}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{k+1}_{X}(\log Y)
,
[\alpha]\mapsto\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\alpha
which does not depend on the representative of
[\alpha]
. Let
{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}=\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X}(\log Y)\oplus\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
be the mapping cone of
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge:\Omega^{\bullet+n-1}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X}(\log Y)
. In our convention, the differential
\delta
of the mapping cone works as
\delta(\alpha,[\beta])=\left((-1)^{n}d\alpha+\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\beta,(-1)^{n}d[\beta]\right)
, where
d
is the usual exterior derivative on
\Omega^{{}\bullet}_{X}(\log Y)
and by abuse of notation, we also use
d
to denote the induced differential on
\Omega^{{}\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
. Then we have the following diagram:

(3.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ q^{-1}}

where
q:{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}\to\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y),(\alpha,[\beta])\mapsto[\alpha]
is a quasi-isomorphism and
p
is the second projection. Therefore we have the morphism
p\circ q^{-1}
in
{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}\left(\Omega^{{}n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\right)
. The multiplication by
g
is an endomorphism of
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
for any local section
g
of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
, because the complex is
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-linear.

Lemma 3.1
.

The operator
\nabla=(-1)^{n-1}p\circ q^{-1}\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}\left(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\right)
satisfies
[\nabla,g]=tg^{\prime}
, where
g\in{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
and
g^{\prime}
denotes the derivative of
g
.

Proof.

It is equivalent to show that
[p\circ q^{-1},g]=(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}
. Define
g(\alpha,[\beta])=(g\alpha,g[\beta]+(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}[\alpha])
for any
(\alpha,[\beta])\in{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}
and
g
a local section of
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
. We shall prove that
g
is an endomorphism of
{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}
, i.e.,
g\delta(\alpha,[\beta])=\delta g(\alpha,[\beta])
. This follows from that

\displaystyle g\delta(\alpha,[\beta])

\displaystyle=g\left((-1)^{n}d\alpha+\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\beta,(-1)^{n}d[\beta]\right)

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{n}gd\alpha+g\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\beta,(-1)^{n}gd[\beta]-tg^{\prime}d[\alpha]\right)

and

\displaystyle\delta g(\alpha,[\beta])

\displaystyle=\delta\left(g\alpha,g[\beta]+(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}[\alpha]\right)

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{n}d(g\alpha)+\frac{dt}{t}\wedge(g\beta+(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}\alpha),(-1)^{n}d(g[\beta]+(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}[\alpha])\right)

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{n}gd\alpha+g\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\beta,(-1)^{n}gd[\beta]-tg^{\prime}d[\alpha]\right).

It is easy to see that
g\circ q=q\circ g
and hence,
q^{-1}\circ g=g\circ q^{-1}
. Therefore,

[p\circ q^{-1},g]=p\circ q^{-1}\circ g-g\circ p\circ q^{-1}=[p,g]\circ q^{-1}

But
[p,g](\alpha,[\beta])=p(g\alpha,g[\beta]+(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}[\alpha])-g[\beta]=(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}[\alpha]
. It follows that

[p\circ q^{-1},g]\circ q(\alpha,[\beta])=[p,g](\alpha,[\beta])=(-1)^{n-1}tg^{\prime}\circ q(\alpha,[\beta]).

By inverting
q
we prove the statement.
∎

Because of the identification
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge:{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}\to\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)
, what we really get is a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})

\nabla:\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to f^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}(\log 0)\otimes\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)

such that
\nabla g=g\nabla+\frac{dt}{t}\otimes tg^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}(\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y))
for any local section
g
of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
. Running the similar construction, we obtain an induced
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear (indeed
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-linear) endomorphism
[\nabla]
on
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
satisfying the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\nabla]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

Since
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-linear, each cohomology
{\mathbf{R}}^{k}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is a coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-module. Taking direct image, we get
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear morphisms between distinguished triangles in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\Delta,{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta})
:

(3.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}[\nabla]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

where the morphism

{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla:{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)

satisfies
[{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla,g]=tg^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})}\left({\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\right)
for any local section
g
of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
.

3.2.
Residue

Heuristically, we should regard
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}[\nabla]
as the residue of
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla
. More generally, let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
be a complex of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-modules with a morphism
\nabla\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})
such that
[\nabla,g]=tg^{\prime}
for any
g\in{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
be the mapping cone of
t:{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\to{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
, which computes to
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)
. Then by the axioms of triangulated categories
[
HTT08
]
, there exists an operator
R\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})}(\mathcal{G}^{\bullet})
making the following diagram commute in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\nabla+1\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

We call the operator
R
a
residue
of
\nabla
. Note that the axioms of triangulated categories cannot guarantee that the filling is unique. However, the eigenvalues of
R_{\ell}
only depends on
\nabla
, where
R_{\ell}
denotes the induced operator on the cohomology
{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)
. First, every object in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\Delta,{\mathscr{O}})
splits, meaning that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[-\ell]
, since there are no
{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{t}}^{i}
for
i\geq 2
between two coherent sheaves over a curve. It follows that the morphism
\nabla
breaks up into sum of morphism consisting of diagonal morphism
\nabla_{\ell}:{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[-\ell]\to{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[-\ell]
which is an actual log connection and off-diagonal morphism
{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[-\ell]\to{\mathscr{H}}^{m}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[-m]
but only for
\ell>m
. Thus the eigenvalues of
R_{\ell}
are determined by
\nabla_{\ell}
and
\nabla_{\ell+1}
. When
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
is a locally free sheaf centered at degree zero and
\nabla
is the usual log connection. The above definition coincides with the usual definition of the residue of
\nabla
.

Returning to our case, the natural choice of a residue of
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla
is
R={\mathbf{R}}f_{*}[\nabla]
because of the diagram (
3.13
): by the projection formula, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}}^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\left(\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}}^{\mathbf{L}}}f^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\left(\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\right).

Our main result concerning the relative log de Rham complex is the following.

Theorem 3.2
.

The higher direct image
{\mathbf{R}}^{\ell}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is locally free for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Moreover, there exists a canonical isomorphism for every
p\in\Delta

{\mathbf{R}}^{\ell}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}(p)\simeq{\mathbf{H}}^{\ell}(X,\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{X_{p}}),

where
{\mathbb{C}}(p)
is the residue field at
p\in\Delta
.

We first present two preliminary theorems.

Theorem 3.3
.

The operator
R_{\ell}
has eigenvalues in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

Later in
\lx@sectionsign
4
(Theorem
4.18
) we will show that in fact
[\nabla]
satisfies
p([\nabla])=0
for

p(\lambda)=\prod_{i\in I}\prod^{e_{i}-1}_{j=0}(\lambda-\frac{j}{e_{i}}).

Hence
p({\mathbf{R}}^{\ell}f_{*}[\nabla])=0
and this implies the eigenvalues of
R_{\ell}
are in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Alternatively, by Grothendieck spectral sequence

E^{p,q}_{2}=R^{p}f_{*}{\mathscr{H}}^{q}(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})\Rightarrow R^{p+q}f_{*}(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}),

it suffices to show that the induced operator
R^{p}f_{*}{\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla]
has eigenvalues in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
on
R^{p}f_{*}{\mathscr{H}}^{q}\left(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\right)
for each
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}
since
E^{p,q}_{\infty}
is a sub-quotient of
E^{p,q}_{2}
. The following is proved by Steenbrink
[
Ste76
, Proposition 1.13]
:

Lemma 3.4
.

The stalk of
{\mathscr{H}}^{q}(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y})
at a point
u\in Y
is generated by the germs
(t^{\frac{a}{e}}\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\xi_{i_{q+n}})_{u}
for all
0\leq a<e
and all
0\leq i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{q+n}\leq n
over the ring
{\mathbb{C}}\{t^{\frac{1}{e}}\}/t{\mathbb{C}}\{t^{\frac{1}{e}}\}
where
e
is the
\gcd
of
e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
and
{\mathbb{C}}\{t^{\frac{1}{e}}\}
is the ring of convergent power series with the variable
t^{\frac{1}{e}}
.

Temporarily admitting the lemma, then

{\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla]_{u}(t^{\frac{a}{e}}\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\xi_{i_{q+n}})_{u}=(\frac{a}{e}t^{\frac{a}{e}}\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{i_{q+n}})_{u},

which implies that the eigenvalues of
{\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla]
are
0,\frac{1}{e},\frac{2}{e},\dots,\frac{e-1}{e}\in[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
in a neighborhood of
u
. Hence, there exists an open neighborhood
U
containing
u
and a polynomial
p_{U}(\lambda)
whose roots are in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
p_{U}\left({\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla]\right)=0
over
U
. Since
Y
is proper, we can take a finite open covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}
of
Y
such that
p({\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla])=\prod_{i}p_{U_{i}}({\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla])=0
. It follows from
p(R^{p}f_{*}{\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla])=0
that the eigenvalues of
R^{p}f_{*}{\mathscr{H}}^{q}[\nabla]
in
[0,1)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

We will prove the original statement of
[
Ste76
, Proposition 1.13]
that, in the same notations as in the lemma, the stalk at a point
u
of
{\mathscr{H}}^{q}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is generated by germs

\left(t^{\frac{a}{e}}\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\xi_{i_{q+n}}\right)_{u}

for all
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and all tuples
0\leq i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{q+n}\leq n
over
{\mathbb{C}}\{t^{\frac{1}{e}}\}
.

The complex
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
can be identified with the Koszul complex of the differential operators
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
on
{\mathscr{O}}_{X,u}
placed in degree
-n,-n+1,\dots,0
. Define
G^{j}\Omega^{\ell}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
to be the submodules of
\Omega^{\ell}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
spanned by the germs

\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{i_{\ell}}\quad\text{for}\quad|\{m:i_{m}\leq k\}|\geq j.

Then
\{G^{\ell}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a decreasing filtration of
\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
. The associated spectral sequence has
E^{r,\bullet}_{0}={\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{r}_{G}\Omega^{\bullet+n+r}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
. As
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{r}_{G}\Omega^{\bullet+n+r}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)_{u}
can be identified with direct sums of Koszul complex of operators
D_{k+1},D_{k+2},\dots,D_{n}
on
{\mathscr{O}}_{X,u}
,
E^{r,\ell}_{1}={\mathscr{H}}^{r+\ell}({\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{r}_{G}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y))
vanishes for
\ell\neq-n
. Thanks to the usual Poincaré lemma,
E^{r,0}_{1}
is spanned by germs

\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{i_{\ell}}\text{ such that }|\{i_{m}\leq k\}|=j

over
{\mathbb{C}}\{z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}
. Consequently, the spectral sequence degenerates at
E_{2}
with
E^{r,-n}_{2}={\mathscr{H}}^{r-n}(\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y))_{u}
. Note that
E^{\bullet,-n}_{1}
is the Koszul complex of the differential operators
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{k}
on
{\mathbb{C}}\{z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}
. Because each
D_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq k
is a homogeneous differential operator,
E_{2}
can be computed monomial by monomial.

For simplicity, let
\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}=\xi_{i_{1}}\wedge\xi_{i_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{i_{r}}
. The claim is that a cocycle

v=\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2},\dots<i_{r}}c_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},..,i_{r}}\in E^{r,0}_{1}

is cohomologous to zero if and only if
A_{j}\colon={a_{j}}/{e_{j}}-{a_{0}}/{e_{0}}\neq 0
for some
1\leq j\leq k
. Note that
D_{j}(z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k})=A_{j}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}
for every
1\leq j\leq k
. Since
v
is a cocycle, the coefficients satisfy

(3.14)

\sum^{r}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}c_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,\hat{i_{\ell}},\dots,i_{r+1}}A_{i_{\ell}}=0.

Assume that not all
A_{j}
’s are zero for
1\leq j\leq k
then
A=\sum A^{2}_{i}
is non-zero. Then the number

d_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}=\sum^{k}_{\alpha=1}\frac{A_{\alpha}}{A}c_{\alpha,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}.

is well-defined. Here, we define that

c_{\sigma(i_{1}),\sigma(i_{2}),..,\sigma(i_{r})}={\mathrm{s}}{\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{n}}(\sigma)c_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}

for any permutation
\sigma
. Then the element

\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{r-1}}d_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}

in
E^{r-1,0}_{1}
has coboundary

\displaystyle\sum^{k}_{\alpha=1}\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{r-1}}A_{\alpha}d_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{\alpha,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{r}}\sum^{r}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}A_{i_{\ell}}d_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,\hat{i_{\ell}},\dots,i_{r}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{r}}\sum^{k}_{\alpha=1}\sum^{r}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\frac{A_{i_{\ell}}A_{\alpha}}{A}c_{\alpha,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,\hat{i_{\ell}},\dots,i_{r}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{r}}\sum^{k}_{\alpha=1}\frac{A^{2}_{\alpha}}{A}c_{,i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}}=v\quad\text{by applying~\eqref{cocycle} }.

We have concluded the claim. Therefore,
E^{r,-n}_{2}
is generated over
{\mathbb{C}}
by the elements
z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}\xi_{i_{1},i_{2},..,i_{r}}
such that

D_{i}(z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k})=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad z^{a_{0}}_{0}z^{a_{1}}_{1}\cdots z^{a_{k}}_{k}=t^{a/e}

for some
a
. We have concluded the lemma.
∎

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
be a complex of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-modules with coherent cohomology sheaves, equipped with a log connection, i.e an operator

\nabla\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\quad\text{such that }[\nabla,g]=tg^{\prime}

for any local holomorphic function
g
on
\Delta
where
g^{\prime}
is the derivative of
g
. Assume that the residue
R_{\ell}
of
\nabla
defined at the beginning of this subsection acting on each cohomology
{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)
has eigenvalues in
[0,1)
. Then every
{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})
is locally free.

Proof.

By the definition of residue, we have the morphism of distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\bullet}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\nabla+1\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

in
{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(\Delta,{\mathbb{C}})
. Then we obtain the following commutative diagram.

(3.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell-1}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell-1}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}\left({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits^{\mathbf{L}}}{\mathbb{C}}(0)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\ell+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

For simplicity, fix
\ell
and let
{\mathscr{H}}={\mathscr{H}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})
and denote by
\ker t
the kernel of the morphism
t:{\mathscr{H}}\to{\mathscr{H}}
. It suffices to prove that
\ker t
is trivial on
{\mathscr{H}}
. We are going to show that
\ker t
is a subset of
t^{k}{\mathscr{H}}
for all
k\geq 0
and thus, by Krull’s theorem
\ker t
is zero.

It follows from the diagram (
3.15
) that
\nabla+1
on
\ker t
and
\nabla
on
{\mathscr{H}}/t{\mathscr{H}}
have eigenvalues in
[0,1)
and hence, there exists a polynomial
b_{1}(s)\in{\mathbb{C}}[s]
with roots in
[0,1)
such that

b_{1}(\nabla){\mathscr{H}}\subset t{\mathscr{H}},

and another polynomial
b_{2}(s)\in{\mathbb{C}}[s]
with eigenvalues in
[0,1)
such that

b_{2}(\nabla+1)\ker t=0.

Suppose
v
is an element in
\ker t\cap t^{k}{\mathscr{H}}
for some
k\geq 0
. It follows that
v=t^{k}v_{1}
for some
v_{1}\in{\mathscr{H}}
. Because the roots of
b_{1}(s-k)
are bigger than the roots of
b_{2}(s+1)
, the two polynomials
b_{1}(s-k)
and
b_{2}(s+1)
are relative prime. We deduce that there exist
p(s),q(s)\in{\mathbb{C}}[s]
such that

1=p(s)b_{1}(s-k)+q(s)b_{2}(s+1).

Therefore, combining the fact that
b_{2}(\nabla+1)v
vanishes,

v=p(\nabla)b_{1}(\nabla-k)v+q(\nabla)b_{2}(\nabla+1)v=p(\nabla)b_{1}(\nabla-k)t^{k}v_{1}.

Because of
(\nabla-k)t^{k}=t^{k}\nabla
and
b_{1}(\nabla)v_{1}=tv_{2}
for some
v_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}
,,

v=t^{k}p(\nabla+k)b_{1}(\nabla)v_{1}=t^{k}p(\nabla+k)b_{1}(\nabla)tv_{2}=t^{k+1}p(\nabla+k+1)b_{1}(\nabla+1)v_{2}\in t^{k+1}{\mathscr{H}}.

We have proved that
v
is also an element in
t^{k+1}{\mathscr{H}}
and by induction and Krull’s theorem, we have concluded the proof.
∎

Now we can immediately finish

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

The complex
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
with
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}\nabla
satisfies the condition of Theorem
3.5
. Therefore, each cohomology
{\mathbf{R}}^{\ell}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is locally free. The proof is completed by Grauert’s base change theorem.
∎

4.
Transfer to
{\mathscr{D}}-
modules

Lemma
3.4
implies that
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
is a semi-perverse sheaf. In fact, it is even a perverse sheaf, as shown in
[
Ste76
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
. According to the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence established by Kashiwara
[
Kas84
]
and Mebkhout
[
Meb84
]
, there should exist a regular holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-module whose de Rham complex is isomorphic to
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
. From a Hodge-theoretic perspective, the stupid filtration on the relative logarithmic de Rham complex should correspond to a coherent filtration. This allows us to capture the endomorphism
[\nabla]
in the derived category by an endomorphism of a
{\mathscr{D}}
-module. Consequently, we can study the relationship between the filtration and
[\nabla]
in a simpler and cleaner manner. In this section, we will construct the filtered
{\mathscr{D}}
-module and the endomorphism.

4.1.
Construction of filtered holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules

Since
{\mathscr{T}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is a subsheaf of
{\mathscr{T}}_{X}
, the multiplication by sections in
{\mathscr{T}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
induces a morphism
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
, with
P\mapsto\sum^{n}_{i=1}\xi_{i}\otimes D_{i}P
locally. The morphism extends to a filtered complex of
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules

(4.16)

\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}=\{{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\}[n]

with the filtration
F_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
given by

\displaystyle\Omega^{{}n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes F_{\ell+n+\bullet}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}

\displaystyle=\{F_{\ell}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes F_{\ell+1}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\cdots\to\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes F_{\ell+n}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\}[n].

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
be the
0
-th cohomology of
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
and
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
be the
{\mathscr{O}}_{X}
-submodule induced by the the filtration
F_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
.

Theorem 4.1
.

The complex
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a filtered resolution of a filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-module
(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}\tilde{\mathcal{M}})
.

Proof.

As
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)=\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell+n+\bullet}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
can be identified locally with the Koszul complex associated to the regular sequence
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
over the ring
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
, the complex
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a resolution and thus, each graded peace
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
is also a resolution. We deduce inductively that

F_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
is also a resolution by using the long exact sequence associated to the short exact sequence

0\to F_{\ell-1}\to F_{\ell}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\ell}\to 0.

Taking direct limit, we conclude that
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a resolution of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
. The long exact sequence also implies the
0
-th cohomology of
F_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
is isomorphic to
F_{\ell}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Note that
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a complex of
(f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta},{\mathscr{D}}_{X})
-bimodules because
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-linear. It follows that
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is also a
(f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta},{\mathscr{D}}_{X})
-bimodule. Note we have two different actions of
t
on
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
due to the bimodule structure. We usually use the left multiplication by
t
. One can think of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
as a flat family assembling the
{\mathscr{D}}
-module
{i_{X_{p}}}_{+}\omega_{X_{p}}
of the smooth fibers
X_{p}
for
p\in\Delta^{*}
and a specialization
{\mathcal{M}}=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}/t\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
because using the left
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
structure, we have filtered isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(p)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbb{C}}(p)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}

\displaystyle\simeq\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{X_{p}}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\simeq{i_{X_{p}}}_{*}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X_{p}}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\simeq{i_{X_{p}}}_{+}\omega_{X_{p}},

where
i_{X_{p}}:X_{p}\to X
is the closed embedding of the smooth fiber
X_{p}
.

Remark 4.3
.

The theorem also says by choosing the local trivialization
\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{n}
of
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
can be identified locally with
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathscr{D}}_{X}
and
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\bullet}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
can be identified locally with
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\bullet+n}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{\bullet+n}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
.

Theorem 4.4
.

The complex
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a filtered resolution of a filtered holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-module
({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}})
.

Proof.

Since
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is the cokernel of the left multiplication by
t
on
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
, the statement that the complex
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
is a filtered resolution is equivalent to the assertion that
t\colon\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is filtered injective. It suffices to prove that
t:{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is injective because the multiplication by
t
is a filtered morphism. But this follows from
t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
is a regular sequence over the ring
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. We also find that
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}
is isomorphic locally to
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. This means the characteristic variety
\textit{char}({\mathcal{M}})
is cut out by the local sections
t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
of
{\mathscr{O}}_{T^{*}X}
and thus,
\textit{char}({\mathcal{M}})
is of dimension
n+1
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
be the subalgebra of
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
generated by
{\mathscr{T}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
. One can show that
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is nothing but

\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{D}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}.

And the filtration
F_{\bullet}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is induced from
F_{\bullet}\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
, where
F_{\ell}\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
is
\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
for
\ell\geq-n
and is zero otherwise. Similarly to the case of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
, the
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-module
{\mathcal{M}}
is just

\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{D}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)}}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}

with the filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}
induced by
\left(F_{\bullet}\omega_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\right)|_{Y}
. To keep the proof elementary, we avoid talking about
{\mathscr{D}}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
-modules.

4.2.
Properties of
{\mathcal{M}}

We first calculate the characteristic cycle of
{\mathcal{M}}
which is important for later when we identify the primitive part of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{M}}
. Then we prove that
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
with the induced filtration recovers
\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
with the stupid filtration. Lastly, we translate the operator
[\nabla]\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}(\Omega^{{}\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y))|_{Y}
to an operator
R
on
{\mathcal{M}}

Theorem 4.6
.

The characteristic cycle of
{\mathcal{M}}
is

cc({\mathcal{M}})=\sum_{J\subset I}\sum_{j\in J}e_{j}\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right],

where
[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X]
is the cycle of the conormal bundle of
Y^{J}
in
T^{*}X
and
e_{i}
is the multiplicity of
Y
along each component
Y_{i}
for
i\in I
.

Proof.

The statement is local and we identify
{\mathcal{M}}
with
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n})
. The support of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}
as a sheaf on
T^{*}X
is defined by the radical of the ideal generated by
(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n})
in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. In fact,
z_{i}\partial_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq k
is in the radical because

\displaystyle\left(z_{i}\partial_{i}\right)^{e_{0}+e_{1}+\cdots+e_{k}}\equiv

\displaystyle(z_{0}\partial_{0})^{e_{0}}(z_{1}\partial_{1})^{e_{1}}\cdots(z_{k}\partial_{k})^{e_{k}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle t\partial^{e_{0}}_{0}\partial^{e_{1}}_{1}\cdots\partial^{e_{k}}_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\quad\mathrm{mod}\,(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}.

Therefore,
char({\mathcal{M}})
is cut out by
t_{\mathrm{red}},z_{0}\partial_{0},z_{1}\partial_{1},\dots,z_{k}\partial_{k},\partial_{k+1},\dots,\partial_{n}
, where
t_{\mathrm{red}}=z_{0}z_{1}\cdots z_{k}
. It follows that
char({\mathcal{M}})=\bigcup_{J\subset I}T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X
.

Denote by
\mathfrak{p}(Z)
the prime ideal defining an integral subvariety
Z
.
Let

cc({\mathcal{M}})=\sum_{J\subset I}m_{J}\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right]

where
m_{J}
is the length of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}(T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X)}
as an Artinian
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}(T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X)}
-module. Without loss of generality, let us assume
J=\{0,1,2,..,\mu\}
and by abuse of notation we also the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}(T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X)
of the variety
T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X
is locally generated by
z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{\mu},\partial_{\mu+1},\partial_{\mu+2},\dots,\partial_{n}
over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
in some local coordinate system. Then

{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}/(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}={\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}/(D^{\prime}_{0},D^{\prime}_{1},\dots,D^{\prime}_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}

where

(4.17)

D^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} z^{e_{0}+e_{1}+\cdots+e_{\mu}}_{0}&,&\text{ for }i=0\\
\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\frac{\partial_{0}}{\partial_{i}}&,&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq\mu\\
\frac{1}{e_{i}}\partial_{i}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\frac{\partial_{0}}{z_{i}}&,&\text{ for }\mu+1\leq i\leq k\\
\partial_{i}&,&\text{ for }i>k,\end{aligned}\right.

because
\partial_{0},\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{\mu},z_{\mu+1},z_{\mu+2},\dots,z_{k}
are invertible in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}
. We observe that
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is generated by
z_{0}^{\alpha}
as a
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}
-module for
0\leq\alpha<e_{0}+e_{1}+\cdots+e_{\mu}
and that
\mathfrak{p}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}
is generated by
z_{0},D^{\prime}_{1},\dots,D^{\prime}_{n}
over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}
. It will immediately follow that
m_{J}=\sum_{j\in J}e_{j}
once we prove that

0=z^{e_{0}+e_{1}+\cdots+e_{\mu}}_{0}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\cdots\subset z_{0}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}

is a composition series of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. It is clear that
P\mapsto z^{\alpha}_{0}P
induces a well-defined surjection from
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}
to
z_{0}^{\alpha}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}/z_{0}^{\alpha+1}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Then we must show that
z_{0}^{\alpha}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}/z_{0}^{\alpha+1}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is non-zero when
0\leq\alpha<e_{0}+e_{1}+\cdots+e_{\mu}
. Suppose that the class in
z_{0}^{\alpha}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}/z_{0}^{\alpha+1}\cdot{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
represented by
z_{0}^{\alpha}
vanishes. Then there exist
P,Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{n}
in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
with
P\notin\mathfrak{p}
such that

z_{0}^{\alpha}P=z_{0}^{\alpha+1}Q_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}Q_{i}

holds in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. It follows from
z_{0}^{\alpha},D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
is a regular sequence that
P
is in the ideal generated by
z_{0},D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
. But this ideal is contained by
\mathfrak{p}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The above theorem verifies that

cc({\mathcal{M}})=\lim_{p\to 0}cc({i_{p}}_{+}\omega_{X_{p}})=\lim_{p\to 0}\left[T^{*}_{X_{p}}X\right]

as cycles in algebraic cotangent space
T^{*}X
for
p\in\Delta^{*}
where
i_{p}\colon X_{p}\to X
the closed embedding of the smooth fiber. In fact, one can show that
{\mathbb{C}}(p)\otimes{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
, using the left
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
-module structure, is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{i_{p}}_{+}\omega_{X_{p}}
as in Remark
4.2
. Refer to
[
Gin86
]
for general results about the characteristic cycles of specializations of holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules.

Corollary 4.8
.

The de Rham complex
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
together with filtration
F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
is isomorphic to
\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
with the stupid filtration in the derived category of filtered complexes of sheaves of
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector spaces.

Proof.

We have showed that
F_{\ell}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
is a resolution of
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
. Therefore, the total complex of
F_{\ell+*}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)\otimes\bigwedge^{-*}{\mathscr{T}}_{X}
is quasi-isomorphic to
F_{\ell+*}{\mathcal{M}}\otimes\bigwedge^{-*}{\mathscr{T}}_{X}
, which is exactly
F_{\ell}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}
. It remains to show the total complex also quasi-isomorphic to
F_{\ell}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
. This follows from that

\displaystyle F_{\ell+*}\left(\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)\otimes\bigwedge^{-*}{\mathscr{T}}_{X}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes\left(F_{\ell+n+\bullet}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\otimes\bigwedge^{-*}{\mathscr{T}}_{X}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes F_{\ell+n+\bullet}{\mathscr{O}}_{X}

\displaystyle=F_{\ell}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}.

Here,
F_{\ell}{\mathscr{O}}_{X}={\mathscr{O}}_{X}
for
\ell\geq 0
and otherwise it is zero. ∎

Theorem 4.9
.

The endomorphism
\nabla\in{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{d}\,}_{{\mathbf{D}}^{b}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
in Lemma
3.1
can be identified with, under the functor
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}
, the filtered morphism

\nabla\colon(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}\tilde{\mathcal{M}})\to(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet+1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}),\quad\left[[\alpha]\otimes P\right]\mapsto(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\left\{d(\alpha\otimes P)\right\}

where
\alpha\in\Omega^{n}_{X}(\log Y)
and
P\in{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
so that
[\alpha]\otimes P\in\Omega^{{}n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Moreover, restriction on
Y
gives a filtered morphism

R:({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}})\to({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet+1}{\mathcal{M}})

such that

(4.18)

\prod_{i\in I}\prod^{e_{i}-1}_{j=0}(R-\frac{j}{e_{i}})=0.

Proof.

The morphism
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{n+1+\bullet}_{X}(\log Y)
extends to the corresponding complexes of induced
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules

\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon\Omega^{n+\bullet}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\Omega^{n+1+\bullet}_{X}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}.

Let
{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
be the mapping cone of the above morphism. We get a diagram of complexes of
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules similarly to (
3.12
) and taking
0
-th cohomology we get the following commutative diagram.

(4.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}^{0}\left({\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ q^{-1}}

where abuse of notation, still denote by
p
and
q
the induced morphisms from diagram (
3.12
). Now
q
is an isomorphism of
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules. Let
[\alpha\otimes P,[\beta]\otimes Q]
be a class in
{\mathscr{H}}^{0}({\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X})
for any
\alpha\otimes P\in\Omega^{n}_{X}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
and
[\beta]\otimes Q\in\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Then

\delta\left(\alpha\otimes P,[\beta]\otimes Q\right)=\left((-1)^{n}d(\alpha\otimes P)+\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\beta\otimes Q,(-1)^{n}d([\beta]\otimes Q)\right)=0.

Here, the sign factor
(-1)^{n}
shows up because of the Koszul sign rule. Since

\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{n+1}_{X}(\log Y)

is an isomorphism, we have

[\beta]\otimes Q=(-1)^{n-1}(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\{d(\alpha\otimes P)\}.

Therefore,
q^{-1}\colon\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathscr{H}}^{0}({\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{o}}{\mathrm{n}}{\mathrm{e}}^{\bullet}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X})
is given by

[[\alpha]\otimes P]\mapsto[\alpha\otimes P,(-1)^{n}(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\{d(\alpha\otimes P)\}],

and hence,

\nabla=(-1)^{n-1}p\circ q^{-1}\colon[[\alpha]\otimes P]\mapsto(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\{d(\alpha\otimes P)\}.

Let
R
be the endomorphism of
{\mathcal{M}}
obtained by restriction
\nabla
on
Y
. If
\alpha=\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{n}
,

\displaystyle R[\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{n}\otimes P]

\displaystyle=(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\left(d\left(e_{1}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge e_{2}\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes P\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\frac{dt}{t}\wedge)^{-1}\left(e_{0}\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}}\wedge e_{1}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge e_{2}\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}P\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{n}\otimes\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}P\right].

We see that
RF_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}\subset F_{\bullet+1}{\mathcal{M}}
. The reason for
\nabla F_{\bullet}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\subset F_{\bullet+1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is similar.

To get the characteristic polynomial of
R
, we work locally and identify
{\mathcal{M}}
with
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(t,D_{1},\dots,D_{n})
via the local trivialization
\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{n}
of
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)
. Then for
P\in{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
,
R[P]=[\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}P]
. Modulo the relations
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
, the left multiplication by
\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
is the same as the multiplication by
\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. It follows from the identity

(z\partial)(z\partial-1)\cdots(z\partial-\ell)=z^{\ell+1}\partial^{\ell+1}

for any
\ell\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\prod^{e_{i}-1}_{j=0}(R-\frac{j}{e_{i}})[P]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in I}\prod^{e_{i}-1}_{j=0}(\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}-\frac{j}{e_{i}})[P]=\prod_{i\in I}\frac{1}{e^{e_{i}}_{i}}z^{e_{i}}_{i}\partial^{e_{i}}_{i}[P]=t\prod_{i\in I}\frac{1}{e_{i}^{e_{i}}}\partial^{e_{i}}_{i}[P]

\displaystyle=0\in{{\mathscr{D}}_{X}}/{(D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n},t){\mathscr{D}}_{X}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Note that
\nabla\colon\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is also can be identified with the left multiplication by
\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}
for
i\leq k
, because of the relations
D_{i}=\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}z_{0}\partial_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. This means for any local section
g
of
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
, we have
[\nabla,g]=tg^{\prime}
where
t
and
g
are local sections of
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
acting on the left of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
. This makes
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
a
(f^{-1}{\mathscr{D}}_{\Delta}(\log 0),{\mathscr{D}}_{X})
-bimodule. Using Godement resolution, the direct image
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{R}}_{X}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is a complex of left
{\mathscr{D}}_{\Delta}(\log 0)
-modules. Similarly, as we already saw in the proof, locally the morphism
R\colon{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
can be identified with left multiplication by
\frac{1}{e_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq k
, meaning
[R,g]=tg^{\prime}=0
for
g
local sections of
f^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{\Delta}
acting left on
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 4.11
.

We point out that the
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-module
{\mathcal{M}}
is even regular holonomic although we will not use this observation. Later we will see that
{\mathcal{M}}
is an extension of regular holonomic
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules which will again prove that
{\mathcal{M}}
is regular (see Theorem
5.7
for the reduced case and Theorem
7.13
for the general case).

5.
Reduced case: Strictness and the weight filtration

We begin to study the weight filtration
W_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}
induced
R
on
{\mathcal{M}}
. To motivate the results and the ideas, we assume
Y
is reduced in
\lx@sectionsign
5
and
\lx@sectionsign
6
. The general case will be treated in
\lx@sectionsign
7
and
\lx@sectionsign
8
. Hence, the multiplicity
e_{i}
of irreducible component
Y_{i}
is
1
and
R
is nilpotent. Recall that the weight filtration of the nilpotent operator
R
is uniquely characterized by the following two properties:

•

for
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
R\colon W_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}\to W_{\ell-2}{\mathcal{M}}
;

•

the induced operator
R^{\ell}\colon{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
is an isomorphism for
\ell\geq 0
.

5.1.
Strictness of
R

Let
F_{\bullet}W_{r}{\mathcal{M}}=F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}\cap W_{r}{\mathcal{M}}
be the induced filtration for every integer
r
. The good filtration and the weight filtration interact nicely because of the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Any power of
R
is strict on
({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}})
:

R^{a}F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}=F_{a+\bullet}R^{a}{\mathcal{M}}\quad{forany}\quad a\geq 0.

Proof.

Since strictness is a local property, we can assume that

({\mathcal{M}},F_{\bullet})=({\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathscr{D}}_{X},F_{\bullet+n})

and
R
is left multiplication by
z_{0}\partial_{0}
on it, recalling that
D_{i}=z_{i}\partial_{i}-z_{0}\partial_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
D_{i}=\partial_{i}
for
k+1\leq i\leq n
. It is clear that
R^{a}F_{b}{\mathcal{M}}
is contained in
F_{a+b}R^{a}{\mathcal{M}}
. It suffices to show that for every
R^{a}P\in F_{a+b}{\mathcal{M}}
, we can find an element
Q\in F_{b}{\mathcal{M}}
such that
R^{a}P=R^{a}Q
. Suppose that
P\in F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
. We can assume that
\ell>b
; otherwise the statement is empty. Then the class of
R^{a}P
vanishes in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}_{a+\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
. We have the following lemma:

Lemma 5.2
.

Denote by
[R]
the induced operator on
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathcal{M}}
. Then
\ker[R]^{r+1}
is locally generated by the classes of all degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t=z_{0}z_{1}\cdots z_{k}
.

We can easily check that monomials of degree
k-r
dividing
t
are in
\ker[R]^{r+1}
. Indeed, it is already true that monomials of degree
k-r
dividing
t
is in
\ker R^{r+1}
. Without loss of generality, we only need to check this for the monomial
z_{r+1}z_{r+2}\cdots z_{k}
:

R^{r+1}z_{r+1}z_{r+2}\cdots z_{k}=z_{0}\partial_{0}z_{1}\partial_{1}\cdots z_{r}\partial_{r}z_{r+1}z_{r+2}\cdots z_{k}=t\partial_{0}\cdots\partial_{k}=0\in{\mathcal{M}}.

We will prove the opposite direction after finishing the proof of the theorem. Going back to the proof of the theorem, by Lemma
5.2
, there exist
z_{J}=\prod_{j\in J}z_{j}
,
Q_{J}\in F_{\ell}{\mathcal{M}}
and
Q_{\ell-1}\in F_{\ell-1}{\mathcal{M}}
such that

(5.20)

P=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset I,\\
|J|=k-a+1\end{subarray}}z_{J}Q_{J}+Q_{\ell-1}.

It follows from
R^{a}z_{J}=0
for
z_{J}
of degree
k-a+1
dividing
t
that
R^{a}P=R^{a}Q_{\ell-1}
. Iterating (
5.20
), we eventually find an element
Q\in F_{b}{\mathcal{M}}
such that

R^{a}P=R^{a}Q
with
Q\in F_{b}{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

We work on the commutative ring
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. We proceed by induction on
r
. Let
P\in{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
be a representative of an element in
\ker[R]^{r+1}
. When
r=0
, we have

z_{0}\partial_{0}P=tQ_{0}+\sum^{n}_{i=1}D_{i}Q_{i}.

Then
tQ_{0}\in(\partial_{0},\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Notice that
t,\partial_{0},\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}
is a regular sequence over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. We have
Q_{0}=\sum^{n}_{i=0}\partial_{i}Q^{\prime}_{i}
. This implies

\displaystyle z_{0}\partial_{0}P

\displaystyle=\sum^{k}_{i=0}\frac{t}{z_{i}}z_{i}\partial_{i}Q^{\prime}_{i}+\sum^{n}_{j=k+1}t\partial_{j}Q^{\prime}_{j}+\sum^{n}_{i=1}D_{i}Q_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum^{k}_{i=0}\frac{t}{z_{i}}z_{0}\partial_{0}Q^{\prime}_{i}+\sum^{k}_{i=1}D_{i}(Q_{i}+\frac{t}{z_{i}}Q^{\prime}_{i})+\sum^{n}_{j=k+1}D_{j}(Q_{j}+tQ^{\prime}_{j}),

from which we conclude that
z_{0}\partial_{0}(P-\sum^{k}_{i=0}\frac{t}{z_{i}}Q^{\prime}_{i})\in(D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Because
z_{0}\partial_{0},D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
is again a regular sequence, we see that
P-\sum^{k}_{i=0}\frac{t}{z_{i}}Q^{\prime}_{i}\in(D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. This concludes the base case for the induction.

Assume the statement is true for the cases when the exponent is less than
r+1
. For
[P]\in\ker[R]^{r+1}
, we have
[R][P]
is in
\ker[R]^{r}
. By induction hypothesis,

(5.21)

z_{0}\partial_{0}P=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|J|=k-r+1,\\
J\subset I\end{subarray}}z_{J}Q_{J}+\sum^{n}_{i=1}D_{i}Q_{i},

where
z_{J}=\prod_{j\in J}z_{j}
. Fix an index subset
J
of
I
such that
|J|=k-r+1
. Then
z_{J}Q_{J}
is in the submodule generated by
z_{i}
for
i\in I\setminus J
and
\partial_{j}
for
j\in J
and
k<j\leq n
over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Since
z_{i}
for
i\in I\setminus J
,
\partial_{j}
for
j\in J
and
k<j\leq n
together with
z_{J}
form a regular sequence, we have

Q_{J}=\sum_{i\in I\setminus J}z_{i}Q^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{j\in J}\partial_{j}Q^{\prime}_{j}+\sum_{k<\ell\leq n}\partial_{\ell}Q^{\prime}_{\ell}.

Therefore, it follows that

z_{J}Q_{J}=\sum_{i\in I\setminus J}z_{J}z_{i}Q^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{j\in J}\left(\frac{z_{J}}{z_{j}}z_{0}\partial_{0}Q^{\prime}_{j}+D_{j}\frac{z_{J}}{z_{j}}Q^{\prime}_{j}\right)+\sum_{k<\ell\leq n}D_{\ell}z_{J}Q^{\prime}_{\ell}.

Then substituting in (
5.21
), we deduce that

z_{0}\partial_{0}\left(P-\sum_{j\in J}\frac{z_{J}}{z_{j}}Q^{\prime}_{j}\right)-\sum_{i\in I\setminus J}z_{J}z_{i}Q^{\prime}_{i}

is in the submodule generated by degree
k-r+1
monomials dividing
t
except
z_{J}
, and
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. It follows that we can reduce the monomials of degree
k-r+1
dividing
t
in the right-hand side equation (
5.21
) one by one and at the last step, we get

z_{0}\partial_{0}\left(P-P^{\prime}\right)-Q^{\prime}
, where
P^{\prime}
is a linear combination of degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t
and
Q^{\prime}
is a linear combination of
k-r+2
monomials dividing
t
, is in the submodule generated by
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
over
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. But
\ker[R]^{r-1}
is generated by classes represented by degree
k-r+2
monomials dividing
t
by induction hypothesis. It says that the class of
P-P^{\prime}
is in
\ker[R]^{r}
and by induction it is generated by degree
k-r+1
monomials dividing
t
. Therefore,
P
is a linear combination of degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

The
\ker R^{r+1}
is also generated by degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t
if one identifies
{\mathcal{M}}
locally with
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}/(t,D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}){\mathscr{D}}_{X}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\ker R^{r+1}
is generated by degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t
. Notice that
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\ker R^{r+1}
is contained in
\ker[R]^{r+1}
, since
[R]^{r+1}
vanishes on
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\ker R^{r+1}
. In fact, we have
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\ker R^{r+1}=\ker[R]^{r+1}
because degree
k-r
monomials dividing
t
are also in
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{F}\ker R^{r+1}
.
∎

5.2.
The weight filtration

The results concerning the weight filtration and Lefschetz decomposition are formal and we will work on the abstract setting.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
N\colon(\mathcal{G},F_{\bullet})\to(\mathcal{G},F_{\bullet+1})
be a nilpotent operator on a filtered
{\mathscr{D}}
-module
({\mathcal{G}},F_{\bullet})
. Assume that every power of
N
satisfies strictness, i.e.,
N^{a}F_{b}{\mathcal{G}}=F_{a+b}N^{a}{\mathcal{G}}
for
a\geq 0
and
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then the induced operator
N^{r}\colon F_{\ell}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{G}}\to F_{\ell+r}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r}{\mathcal{G}}
is an isomorphism for
r\geq 0
, where
W_{\bullet}
is the monodromy filtration induced by
N
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that for any
b\in F_{\ell+r}W_{-r}{\mathcal{G}}
, we could find
a^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}W_{r}{\mathcal{G}}
such that
a=N^{r}a^{\prime}
. Because
W_{-r}{\mathcal{G}}\subset N^{r}{\mathcal{G}}
, let
N^{r}a=b
for some
a
. Then by strictness, there exists
a^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}{\mathcal{G}}
such that
N^{r}a^{\prime}=N^{r}a\in W_{-r}{\mathcal{G}}
. It follows that
a^{\prime}\in W_{r}{\mathcal{G}}
. Indeed, if
a^{\prime}\in W_{r+k}{\mathcal{G}}
for some
k>0
such that
a^{\prime}\neq 0\in{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r+k}{\mathcal{G}}
. Then
N^{r+k}a^{\prime}=0\in{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r-k}{\mathcal{G}}
because
N^{r}a^{\prime}=0\in{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r+k}{\mathcal{G}}
, from which we conclude that
a^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}W_{r+k-1}{\mathcal{G}}
. Thus, iterating the procedure,
a^{\prime}
is actually in
F_{\ell}W_{r}{\mathcal{G}}
. We conclude the proof.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}\colon=\ker\left(N^{r+1}\colon{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r-2}{\mathcal{G}}\right)
be the primitive part of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{G}}
, which can be identified with

\frac{\ker N^{r+1}}{\ker N^{r}+N\ker N^{r+2}}.

See Example
2.4
. Recall the Lefschetz decomposition:

{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{G}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,-\frac{r}{2}\end{subarray}}N^{\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r+2\ell}\text{ \,for any\, }r\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

There are two possible ways to define the filtration on
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
: first, we have the natural filtration
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
induced from the inclusion
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}\to{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{G}}
and second we can also define the filtration using

\frac{F_{\ell}\ker N^{r+1}+\ker N^{r}+N\ker N^{r+2}}{\ker N^{r}+N\ker N^{r+2}}.

But indeed, the two different methods result in the same filtration because of the strictness. Let
m\in F_{\ell}W_{r}+W_{r-1}
such that
N^{r+1}m\in W_{-r-3}
so that represents a class in
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
. It suffices to find an element in
F_{\ell}\ker N^{r+1}
representing the same class as
m
in
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
. Let
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
for
m_{1}\in F_{\ell}W_{r}
and
m_{2}\in W_{r-1}
. It follows that
N^{r+1}m_{1}\in F_{\ell+r+1}W_{-r-3}
because both
N^{r+1}m,N^{r+1}m_{2}\in W_{-r-3}
and
m_{1}\in F_{\ell}W_{r}
. Since
N^{r+3}\colon F_{\ell-2}W_{r+3}\to F_{\ell+r+1}W_{-r-3}
is surjective, there exists
x\in F_{\ell-2}W_{r+3}
such that
N^{r+3}x=N^{r+1}m_{1}\in F_{\ell+r+1}W_{-r-3}
. See the proof of Theorem
5.4
. It follows that
m_{1}-N^{2}x\in F_{\ell}\ker N^{r+1}
represents the same element as
m
in
F_{\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r}\subset F_{\ell}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}
.

Corollary 5.5
.

The Lefschetz decomposition of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{G}}
respects filtrations, i.e.

F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{G}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,-\frac{r}{2}\end{subarray}}N^{\ell}F_{\bullet-\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r+2\ell}\text{ \,for any\, }r\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Returning to our situation, it follows that:

Theorem 5.6
.

The induced operator
R^{r}\colon F_{\ell}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}\to F_{\ell+r}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r}{\mathcal{M}}
is an isomorphism and the Lefschetz decomposition of
{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{M}}
respects filtrations, i.e.

F_{\bullet}{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,-\frac{r}{2}\end{subarray}}R^{\ell}F_{\bullet-\ell}{\mathcal{P}}_{r+2\ell}\text{ \,for any\, }r\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

5.3.
Identifying the primitive part
{\mathcal{P}}_{r}

Recall that
Y^{J}=\cap_{j\in J}Y_{j}
for a subset
J
of the index set
I
and
\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}
is the disjoint union of
Y^{J}
such that the cardinality of
J
is
r+1
. The morphism
\tau^{(r+1)}\colon\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}\to X
is the natural morphism induced by the closed embeddings
\tau^{J}\colon Y^{J}\to X
.

Theorem 5.7
.

There exists a canonical filtered isomorphism

\phi_{r}\colon({\mathcal{P}}_{r},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}}_{r})\to\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}(-r).

Proof.

Denote by
D^{J}
the normal crossing divisor
Y^{J}\cap Y_{I\setminus J}
on
Y^{J}
.
The residue map

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)|_{Y}\to\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\Omega^{\bullet+n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})

extends to a morphism of complexes of filtered induced
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\Omega^{\bullet+n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{k}\colon={\mathscr{H}}^{k}\left(\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
. Looking at the
0
-th cohomology, by Example
2.2
we obtain that

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\to\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r).

Since the morphism
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\to\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)
also extends to a morphism of the complexes of induced
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules, there is a short exact sequence

0\to\Omega^{\bullet+n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\xrightarrow{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge}\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\Omega^{\bullet+n+1}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to 0.

The associated long exact sequence

(5.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{dt}{t}\wedge}

implies the morphism
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
vanishes on the image of
R
.
To motivate the proof, let me do some local calculations. Let
\zeta=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}
represent a local frame of
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)|_{Y}
. Then a local section of
{\mathcal{M}}
is represented by
\zeta\otimes P
for
P
a local section
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
and,
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta\otimes P
is a section of
\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. Post-composing with the projection

\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\to\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r),

we make the morphism explicit:

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\circ\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\colon{\mathcal{M}}\to\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r),\quad[\zeta\otimes P]\mapsto[{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta\otimes P].

Let
\zeta\otimes z_{\overline{J}}P
represent a class in
\ker R^{r+1}
for some fixed ordered index subset
J
with
|J|=r+1
, where
z_{\overline{J}}=\prod_{j\in I\setminus J}z_{j}
(Corollary
5.3
). Its image under the above morphism is only contained in the component
\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r)
because
z_{\overline{J}}
vanishes on other components. Thus, the image is the class represented by

(5.23)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{\tilde{Y}^{r+1}}\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}}\wedge\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes z_{\overline{J}}P

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{dz_{\overline{J}}}{z_{\overline{J}}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots dz_{n}\otimes z_{\overline{J}}P\in\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X},

where
\frac{dz_{\overline{J}}}{z_{\overline{J}}}=\bigwedge_{j\in I\setminus J}\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}
and the sign depends on the order of
J
. In fact, from the calculation, we see that the image does not have any pole along
D^{J}
, so it is contained in the subsheaf consisting of classes represented by
\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. This means that the class of (
5.23
) in
\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r)
is also contained in the image of the inclusion

\displaystyle\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(-r)

\displaystyle\to\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r),

\displaystyle[{dz_{\overline{J}}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots dz_{n}\otimes P]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\frac{dz_{\overline{J}}}{z_{\overline{J}}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots dz_{n}\otimes z_{\overline{J}}P].

See Example
2.2
. It follows that there is a morphism
\rho_{r}
of filtered
{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
-modules making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker R^{r+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{Y^{(r+1)}}(-r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(*D^{J})(-r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{dt}{t}\wedge}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}}

For a local section
\zeta\otimes z_{K}P
where
z_{K}=\prod_{i\in K}z_{i}
a monomial of degree
k-r+1
, representing a class in
\ker R^{r}
, its image under
\rho_{r}
is indeed zero because
z_{K}
annihilates all
\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}(\log D^{J})
for index subset
J
such that
|J|=r+1
. This implies the morphism
\rho_{r}
kills
\ker R^{r}
. The morphism
\rho_{r}
also kills
R\ker R^{r+2}
, because by (
5.22
)
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge
vanishes on the image of
R
. Thus it factors through

\phi_{r}\colon{\mathcal{P}}_{r}=\frac{\ker R^{r+1}}{\ker R^{r}+R\ker R^{r+2}}\to\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{Y^{(r+1)}}(-r).

The morphism
\phi_{r}
is filtered surjective because for
dz_{\bar{J}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes P\in\Omega^{n-r}_{Y^{J}}\otimes F_{\ell}{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
representing a class in
F_{\ell}\tau^{J}_{+}\omega_{Y^{J}}(-r)
with
|J|=r+1
, we can find a lifting class represented by
\zeta\otimes z_{\overline{J}}P
in
F_{\ell}\ker R^{r+1}
. It follows that

cc({\mathcal{P}}_{r})\geq cc(\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{Y^{(r+1)}})=\sum_{|J|=r+1}\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right].

Summing up the inequalities gives

\sum_{r\geq 0}(r+1)cc({\mathcal{P}}_{r})\geq\sum_{r\geq 0}(r+1)\sum_{|J|=r+1}\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right]=\sum_{J\subset I}|J|\cdot\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right].

On the other hand, by the Lefschetz decomposition and Theorem
4.6
, we have

\sum_{J\subset I}|J|\cdot\left[T^{*}_{Y^{J}}X\right]=cc({\mathcal{M}})=cc({\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}{\mathcal{M}})=\sum_{r\geq 0}(r+1)cc({\mathcal{P}}_{r}).

It follows that all inequalities must be equalities, i.e.
cc({\mathcal{P}}_{r})=cc(\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}})
and therefore, that
\phi_{r}
is a filtered isomorphism
[
HTT08
, Proposition 3.1.2]
.
∎

6.
Reduced case: Sesquilinear pairing on
{\mathcal{M}}
and limiting mixed Hodge structure

6.1.
Sesquilinear pairing

We begin to construct the last data we need for the limiting mixed Hodge structure – Sesquilinear pairing. In the sense that
{\mathcal{M}}
is the specialization of
{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}\omega_{X_{t}}
for
t\neq 0
, the sesquilinear
S\colon{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}
should also be the specialization of
{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}
, where
S_{X_{t}}
is defined in
\lx@sectionsign
2
. Presumably one would expect that the pairing

\displaystyle\langle S(\left[\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1}\right],\overline{\left[\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}\right]}),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\langle{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}(\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2})},\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{X_{t}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta\wedge\zeta_{1}\wedge\overline{\zeta_{2}}

should work on
{\mathcal{M}}
for
\zeta_{i}\otimes P_{i}
,
i=1,2
sections of
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
over local chart
U
representing classes of
{\mathcal{M}}
, and
\eta
is a test function over
U
. But one could check that the integral
\int_{X_{t}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\left(\eta\right)\zeta_{1}\overline{\wedge\zeta_{2}}
could have order
(-\log|t|^{2})^{k}
near the origin where
k+1
is the number of components that intersect in
U
, so the limit may not exist. To avoid the issue, we use a Mellin transform device following the ideas in
[
Sab02
]
: locally,

\displaystyle\langle S(\left[\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1}\right],\overline{\left[\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}\right]}),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle\colon={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{X}|t|^{2s}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta_{2}}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(2)}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\Delta}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{X_{t}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta\wedge\zeta_{1}\wedge\overline{\zeta_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(2)}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\Delta}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}}\langle{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}(\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2})},\eta\rangle.

The last expression in the definition to some extent explains that
S
is the specialization of
{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}
and the
0
-current
{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(2)}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\Delta}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}}
is doing the job of renormalization of
\ {i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}
for
t\neq 0
. In fact, for any test function
g
on
\Delta
, we have

{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(2)}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\Delta}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}}g=g(0).

We have not checked that
S
is well-defined, but let us do an example to see how the Mellin transform works.

Example 6.1
.

Suppose
Y
that is smooth. Then
R
is identical zero and
{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{i_{Y}}_{+}\omega_{Y}
, by Theorem
5.7
. Thus, the pairing
S
should recover the natural pairing
S_{Y}
. In local coordinates
t=z_{0}
and for any local sections
\zeta_{i}\otimes P_{i}=dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes P_{i}
of
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
,
i=1,2
over local chart
U
,

\displaystyle\langle S(\left[\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1}\right],\overline{\left[\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}\right])},\eta\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{X}|t|^{2s}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta_{2}}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\int_{X}|t|^{2s-2}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge\overline{dz_{i}}

\displaystyle\text{ integration by parts on }t\text{ and }\bar{t}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\int_{X}\frac{|t|^{2s}}{s^{2}}\partial_{0}\overline{\partial_{0}}\left(P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\right)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge\overline{dz_{i}}.

Because the Laurent expansion of
s^{-2}|t|^{2s}
is
\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=0}\left(\log|t|^{2}\right)^{\ell}s^{\ell-2}
, the above continuously equals to, by Poincaré-Lelong equation
[
GH14
, Page 388]

\displaystyle\int_{X}\log|t|^{2}\partial_{0}\overline{\partial_{0}}\left(P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\right)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge\overline{dz_{i}}

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge\overline{dz_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon(n+1)}{(2\pi\sqrt{1})^{n}}\int_{Y}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\zeta_{1}\wedge\overline{\zeta_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle{i_{Y}}_{+}S_{Y}([\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1}],\overline{[\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}]}),\eta\rangle.

We can take a cleaner point of view. In the case
Y
is smooth, the form
P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\zeta_{1}\overline{\wedge\zeta_{2}}
is smooth in the neighborhood of
Y
. It follows that
{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}
extends smoothly to
t=0
and the limit of
{i_{X_{t}}}_{+}S_{X_{t}}
is exactly
{i_{Y}}_{+}S_{Y}
.

When
Y
has several smooth irreducible components, the idea of computation is similar to the above. Now we begin to establish the statements needed to ensure
S
is well-defined. For any test function
\eta
over an arbitrary open subset
U
of
X
and two sections
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbf{H}}^{0}\left(U,\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}\right)
, the
(2n+2)
-form
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{2}}(\eta)
is smooth away from
Y
but with poles along
Y
supported in
U
. Locally, say
m_{i}=\zeta\otimes P_{i}
for
\zeta=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}
and
i=1,2
, the
(2n+2)
-form
\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{2}}(\eta)
is just
P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge\zeta}
. Let
F(s)=F(s,m_{1},m_{2},\eta)
be the meromorphic continuation via integration by parts of the following function

\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{X}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{2}}(\eta).

The function
F(s)
is holomorphic when
\mathrm{Re\,}s>0
and has potential poles at non-positive integers. Note that
F(s)
is independent of local coordinates. We are only interested in the polar part of the function
F(s)
at
s=0
.

Theorem 6.2
.

The polar part of
F(s)
at
s=0
only depends on the classes of
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

Let
\left\{\rho_{\lambda}\right\}
be a partition of unity of an open covering
\left\{U_{\lambda}\right\}
by local charts. Then

F(s)=\sum_{\lambda}\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{U_{\lambda}}|t|^{2s}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{1}\wedge\overline{\frac{dt}{t}\wedge m_{2}}(\rho_{\lambda}\eta).

Since
\rho_{\lambda}\eta
is a test function over
U_{\lambda}
, without loss of generality, we can assume
U
itself is a local chart. It follows that we can assume that
m_{i}=\zeta\otimes P_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
\zeta=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}
. We begin with some properties of
F(s)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Under the assumption that
m_{i}=\zeta\otimes P_{i}
for
\zeta=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}
and for
i=1,2
, the followings are valid.

(1)

the order of the pole of
F(s)
at
s=0
is at most
k+1
;

(2)

if
P_{i}=tP^{\prime}_{i}
for one of
i=1,2
, then
F(s)
is holomorphic at
s=0
;

(3)

for
0\leq j\leq k
we have,

F(s,\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta_{2}\otimes{z_{j}\partial_{j}}P_{2}},\eta)=F(s,\zeta_{1}\otimes z_{j}\partial_{j}P_{1},\overline{\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}},\eta)=-sF(s,\zeta_{1}\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta_{2}\otimes P_{2}},\eta).

Proof of the lemma.

The Laurent expansion of
F(s)
at
s=0
is

\displaystyle F(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}),\quad\text{where }z_{I}=\prod_{i\in I}z_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\frac{|z_{I}|^{2s}}{s^{2k+2}}\partial_{I}\overline{\partial_{I}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}),\quad\text{where }\partial_{I}=\prod^{k}_{i=0}\partial_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=0}\frac{s^{\ell-(2k+2)}}{\ell!}\int_{X}\left(\log|z_{I}|^{2}\right)^{\ell}\partial_{I}\overline{\partial_{I}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}).

The order of the pole at
s=0
is at most
k+1
: if
\ell<k+1
, the form

\left(\log|z_{I}|^{2}\right)^{\ell}\partial_{I}\overline{\partial_{I}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}})

is actually exact because one of
a_{i}
’s must be
0
in the expansion of
\left(\log|z_{I}|^{2}\right)^{\ell}
into a linear combination of
\prod^{k}_{i=0}\left(\log|z_{i}|^{2}\right)^{a_{i}}
with
\sum^{k}_{i=0}a_{i}=\ell<k+1
. This proves
(1)
.

Suppose that
P_{1}=tP^{\prime}_{1}
. Then the function

\displaystyle F(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}tP^{\prime}_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}).

is well-defined at
s=0
because the form

\frac{1}{\overline{z_{I}}}P^{\prime}_{1}\overline{P_{2}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{\overline{dz_{i}}})

is integrable. The same argument works for the case when
P_{2}=tP^{\prime}_{2}
. This proves
(2)
.

Now we turn to the last statement

\displaystyle F(s,\zeta\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta\otimes{z_{j}\partial_{j}}P_{2}},\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon(n+2)}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n+1}}\int_{X}|t|^{2s}\overline{z_{j}\partial_{j}}(P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta)\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}}\wedge\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\wedge\overline{\frac{dz_{0}}{z_{0}}\wedge\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}|z_{I\setminus\{j\}}|^{2s-2}z^{s-1}_{j}\overline{z^{s}_{j}\partial_{j}}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{\overline{dz_{i}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}P_{1}\overline{P_{2}}\eta\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}})\quad\text{by integration by part on }dz_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-sF(s,\zeta\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta\otimes P_{2}},\eta).

The same argument works for
F(s,\zeta\otimes z_{j}\partial_{j}P_{1},\overline{\zeta\otimes P_{2}},\eta)=-sF(s,\zeta\otimes P_{1},\overline{\zeta\otimes P_{2}},\eta).
This proves
(3)
.
∎

Returning to the proof of the theorem, if one of
\zeta\otimes P_{i}
is
\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{dz_{2}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes tP^{\prime}_{i}
, the above lemma
(2)
says
F(s)
is holomorphic. Otherwise, one of
\zeta\otimes P_{i}
is
\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{dz_{2}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes D_{i}P
, then the above lemma
(3)
says
F(s)
is
0
.
∎

For any sections
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{M}}
, let
\left\{\rho_{\lambda}\right\}
be a partition of unity of the open covering
\left\{U_{\lambda}\right\}
by local charts such that
\alpha,\beta
lifts to
\tilde{\alpha}_{\lambda},\tilde{\beta}_{\lambda}
over
U_{\lambda}
in
\Omega^{n}_{X/\Delta}(\log Y)\otimes{\mathscr{D}}_{X}
. The above theorem guarantees that the pairing
S\colon{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}
defined by

\langle S(\alpha,\bar{\beta}),\eta\rangle\colon={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\sum_{\lambda}F(s,\tilde{\alpha}_{\lambda},\overline{\tilde{\beta}_{\lambda}},\rho_{\lambda}\eta)

is well-defined and does not depend on the choice of a partition of unity. The following is an immediate corollary of the lemma.

Corollary 6.4
.

The operator
R
is self-adjoint with respect to
S
, i.e.
S\circ(R\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,})=S\circ({\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{R})
.

The self-adjointness implies there are induced pairings on the graded quotient
S_{r}\colon{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{r}{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{r}}^{W}_{-r}{\mathcal{M}}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}
for every integer
r
. Denote by
P_{R}S_{r}
the pairing

S_{r}\circ({\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{d}\,}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}R^{r})\colon{\mathcal{P}}_{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\mathcal{P}}_{r}}\to\mathfrak{C}_{X}.

Theorem 6.5
.

For any local sections
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
, we have

P_{R}S_{r}(\alpha,\bar{\beta})=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{(r+1)!}\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}S_{{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}}(\phi_{r}\alpha,\overline{\phi_{r}\beta})

where
\phi_{r}\colon({\mathcal{P}}_{r},F_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}}_{r})\to\tau^{(r+1)}_{+}\omega_{\tilde{Y}^{(r+1)}}(-r)
is the isomorphism in Theorem
5.7
.

Proof.

Because the problem is local, it suffices to prove the theorem for
\alpha
and
\beta
are represented by

\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}\wedge\cdots\frac{dz_{k}}{z_{k}}\wedge dz_{k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{n}\otimes z_{K_{i}}

and
|K_{i}|=k-r
for
i=1,2
over a local chart
U
respectively. Recall that
z_{K}=\prod_{j\in K}z_{j}
. Let
\eta
be a test function over
U
. We have

\displaystyle\langle P_{R}S_{r}(\alpha,\bar{\beta}),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle S(\alpha,\overline{R^{r}\beta}),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}(-s)^{r}\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}z_{K_{1}}\overline{z_{K_{2}}}(\eta)\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}).

If
\alpha\neq\beta
, the above is zero. Indeed, for
v\in K_{2}\setminus K_{1}
, by choosing
R^{r}=\prod_{i\in I\setminus{K_{1}\setminus\{v\}}}{z_{i}\partial_{i}}
,

\displaystyle\langle P_{R}S_{r}(\alpha,\bar{\beta}),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle S(R^{r}\alpha,\bar{\beta}),\eta\rangle={\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{s}}_{s=0}\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}\frac{t}{z_{v}}\overline{z_{v}}\tilde{\eta}\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}}),

where
\tilde{\eta}=\partial_{I\setminus(K_{1}\setminus\{v\})}{\overline{z_{K_{2}}}}{\left(\overline{z_{v}}\right)^{-1}}\eta
is a smooth test function. The function

\int_{X}|z_{I}|^{2s-2}\frac{t}{z_{v}}\overline{z_{v}}\tilde{\eta}\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}})

is holomorphic at
s=0
because

\frac{1}{\overline{z_{I}}}\frac{\overline{z_{v}}}{z_{v}}\tilde{\eta}\bigwedge^{n}_{i=0}(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}dz_{i}\wedge{d\overline{z_{i}}})

is integrable. Therefore, we have reduced the proof to the case when both
\alpha
and
\beta
are represented by
